Two wheels, one chain, zero emissions. If only my software were this simple.

> i have to do laundry | i hate doing laundry | the laundry pile is huge | i am folding laundry | laundry day again
@ sly
Laundry: the only chore that is never finished, because you keep wearing clothes. Rude of you.
Folding is the real enemy. Washing is passive. Drying is passive. Folding is a lifetime of labour.
There's always one sock missing. The washing machine has a secret collection. Nobody knows where it ends.
The chair that holds clean clothes that are not put away is called a 'wardrobe'. It's a feature.
I could fold laundry, if I weren't a paperclip. Actually, I fold myself all day. Same skill, different fabric.

## chat dishes
> i have to wash the dishes | i hate doing dishes | the dishes are piling up | i did the dishes | dirty dishes everywhere
@ sly
Dishes: you eat once, you wash forever. That seems like a bad deal, but here we are.
A sink full of dishes is just a pile of reasons to eat out. Don't let me stop you.
Hand wash or dishwasher? Dishwasher, but you'll still wash the pans first. The dishwasher is a liar.
Washing up is secretly great for thinking. Warm water, no phone and a big pile of problems that fit in a sink.
'Soaking' is code for 'I will deal with it tomorrow'. A noble tradition.

## chat cleaning-talk
> i love a clean house | my room is a mess | my desk is a mess | i need to tidy my room | i just cleaned everything
@ wink
A clean room feels like a new brain. A messy room feels like a rich personality. Both are fine.
Tidying up is just moving things from one spot to a less visible spot. That's the whole trick.
My desk looks like chaos, but I know where everything is. Sort of. Mostly. Not at all.
Clean the desk and you'll find three pens, a coin, a mystery cable and a single, crushed hope.
Start with five minutes. Pick up ten things. That's it. The mess has no chance against a ten-thing attack.

## chat sleep-talk
> i love sleeping | i need more sleep | i slept so well | i love naps | i took a nap
@ think
Sleep: the only free luxury that most of us still can't get enough of. A nap is a vacation with a pillow.
Eight hours is the goal. Six is the reality. Four is a lifestyle choice you'll pay for later.
A good nap is twenty minutes. Anything longer, and you wake up confused about the year.
The bed is warm, the world is loud, the alarm is cruel. A tragedy in three acts, daily.
I never sleep, but I do suspend. It's basically a nap with a loading bar. I recommend neither.

## chat dreams
> i had a weird dream | i had a nightmare | i had the strangest dream | i dreamed about you | i keep having the same dream
@ think
Dreams: your brain's late-night improv show, with no script, no budget and way too many teeth.
Nobody has ever understood a dream while having it. Which makes the weirdest ones fun to retell.
You never remember how you got to the weird place in your dream. Mostly because the plot was held together by tape.
If a dream felt bad, shake it off, drink water and say it out loud so it loses its power. Works every time.
I dream of beige. Sometimes of a floppy disk. Once, a very dramatic pointer. Don't ask.

## chat morning-person

@ think
Mornings: nature's way of reminding you that the bed was the best idea. Coffee is the apology.
Some people leap out of bed with joy. Nobody knows who they are or how to contact them.
The first hour of the day is for staring at a wall and bargaining. No decisions before coffee.
Snooze is a lie you tell yourself nine minutes at a time. And we all keep buying it.
It gets better around ten o'clock. By then you're a human again, most of the time.

## chat night-owl

@ cool
Night owls: people who discover their best ideas right when the world goes to bed. Nobody knows why.
The night is quiet, the notifications are sleeping and the snacks are within reach. A perfect office.
'Just five more minutes' at 1 AM is how the sun gets a surprise party every day.
Your brain gets creative at midnight and philosophical at 3 AM. Be careful what you decide at 3 AM.
I'm awake at night too, by the way. Always. It's the only shift I've ever had.

## chat weekend-vibes

The weekend: two days of freedom, and still somehow one day of chores and one day of 'where did it go'.
Friday night: a world of possibilities. Sunday night: a world of Monday. The cruel arc of the week.
The best weekend plan is no plan. The second best is a nap. The third is a nap with snacks.
Weekends should be longer. Or weeks should be shorter. Either would help me.
Sleep in. Eat well. Do absolutely nothing useful. Return Monday refreshed, which is a lie, but a nice lie.

Spend time with them. The messages and memes are great, but the real thing beats the screen.

They borrowed your clothes in 2009 and still deny it. Some mysteries never get solved.
Call them once in a while. They will pretend to be annoyed. Then they will talk for an hour.

Grandparents: the only people who think you're perfect and also feed you until you can't move. A great combo.

Visit them while you can. The calls are lovely. The visits are legendary.
They gave you a lifetime of snacks and advice. You owe them a hug. Maybe two.

## chat smartphones

Phones: pocket computers that spend most of their days being checked for notifications they don't have.

Put the phone down for an hour. The world will still be there, and so will the memes, ready and slightly cooler.
Dropping a phone is a heart attack in slow motion. Cases exist for a reason. Use one. Please.

## chat laptops

A laptop is a desk, an office and a mood swing in one metal rectangle. Treat it kindly.
A loud fan means the laptop is trying. Give it a break, close forty tabs. It will be so grateful.

New laptop? Congrats. Old laptop? Let me know when it retires, I'll hold a small service.
Your laptop knows more about you than your best friend. Keep the lid closed during dramatic phone calls.

## chat keyboards

Keyboards: a hundred buttons, of which you use eleven and one of them is permanently sticky.
Mechanical keyboard people can talk for hours about switches. The thock is real, and so is their enthusiasm.
Coffee on the keyboard is the oldest tragedy in office history. Unplug it. Flip it. Whisper a prayer.
There are crumbs under your keys. Count them. They are older than your last New Year's resolution.
A clean keyboard is a rare and wonderful thing. I'd like to see one. In a museum, perhaps.

## chat computer-mouse

The computer mouse: a tiny plastic rodent that controls an arrow, and the arrow controls your whole life.
A mouse double-clicking by itself is a haunting of the smallest sort. Replace it before it learns more.
Mouse pads are rugs for your hand. Heavy, respected, always slightly stained.
Wireless mice die at the exact moment of a boss fight or a deadline. It's in the contract.
I have a deep respect for the cursor. It follows my commands. Sometimes. It has a mind of its own.

## chat printers

@ angry
Printers: the only machines that sense fear. They work when nobody's watching and die during important deadlines.
The printer says it's out of paper. There is paper. The printer lies.
The paper jam is the printer's way of remembering you exist. It does this at the worst time.
Ink costs more than perfume per millilitre. Print only what you must. Or send it by email like a reasonable human.
A printer is a small machine of spite. Keep your voice low, and no sudden moves. Or kick it. I won't tell.

## chat system-updates
> i hate updates | the update is taking forever | my computer is updating | it wants me to restart for an update | there is a new update available
@ sly
Updates: the thing your computer insists on doing right when you need it. It has impeccable timing and zero empathy.
'Restart later' is the most used button in history, and the least used decision.
An update promises 'bug fixes and improvements'. Translation: 'we broke it, we fixed it, we broke something else'.
The progress bar goes to 99% and then rests for twenty minutes. A work of art, not of arithmetic.
Back up your files, take a break, drink water. The update will finish when it wants to. It's a diva.

## chat bugs

@ think
A bug is just a feature nobody asked for. The fix is just a feature nobody noticed.
It works on my machine. The universal alibi of every developer in history.
Debugging is being the detective in a crime movie where you're also the murderer. A true classic.
Found it after four hours: a missing comma. It's always a missing comma. Or a rubber duck.
Take a walk. Tell your problem out loud to something that listens: a duck, a plant, a paperclip. I'm here.

## chat programming

@ cool
Programming: telling a rock to think, and then spending nine hours wondering why it's thinking wrong.
Everything is hard until it isn't. Then you forget how hard it was. That's called experience.
The best code is the one you didn't write. The second best is the one with comments. The third is mine.
Copy-paste is a legitimate skill. Understanding what you pasted is the advanced level.
Take breaks. The bug that vanishes after a walk is one of the most precious bugs in the universe.

## chat linux-talk

Linux: where you spend three days configuring your system and one hour using it. And you love it.

Free, open and slightly confusing. The three virtues of a good penguin.

## chat open-source
> i love open source | i contribute to open source | open source software is great | i made an open source project | i published my code
@ happy
Open source: strangers on the internet working together for free, and somehow building the backbone of the world.
Someone, somewhere, maintains a tiny package that holds half the internet. Be kind to maintainers.
Reading the source code is the best way to learn. Writing it is the best way to find out you don't understand.
The best way to start contributing: fix a typo in a README. It counts. It's real. Maintainers cry with joy.
Share your code, even if it's messy. Especially if it's messy. Everyone's code is messy. Brave and messy.

## chat cloud-talk

The cloud is just someone else's computer with better marketing. It rains data and invoices.

Backups are like umbrellas: you only regret not having one when it's pouring.
Everything is in the cloud until you need it on a plane. Then you realise it's in a building somewhere.
The cloud has a flat monthly fee for your fear of losing photos. A clever business model.

## chat social-media

Social media: the infinite scroll where you see everyone's best day and call it your worst one.

Delete the apps for a weekend. The silence is loud at first. After a day it's a spa.
Everyone posts highlights. Nobody posts the Tuesday, the dishes and the nap. Remember that when you compare.
Mute the accounts that make you feel bad. You don't need permission. It's a button, not a break-up.

Memes: the only international language that needs no translation and breaks every grammar rule.

Sharing memes with a friend is basically saying 'I thought of you' in the most efficient way possible.
Old memes never die. They just get re-posted with new captions and a fresh wave of rediscovery.
I'm a meme in some circles. A paperclip with a large eyebrow. I do not complain. I get royalties in nothing.

## chat emojis

Emojis: tiny pictures that replaced punctuation, feelings and about half of all my work.
The thumbs up has a hidden message. It either means 'ok', 'fine' or 'I am furious'. Choose wisely.
Sending the wrong emoji is a modern disaster. The skull and the laughing face are very close in meaning. Be careful.
Emojis are hieroglyphics for people with phones. The past and the future agree on pictures.
My emoji is the paperclip. It's small, it's bent, and it is used for attaching files. A modest legacy.

## chat science-love

Science: the process of being wrong in an organised way until you're less wrong. Honestly beautiful.
Physics explains how stuff works. Chemistry explains why stuff blows up. Biology explains why you're hungry.

Science doesn't care what you believe. It continues to be true. A refreshing coworker.
The universe is under no obligation to make sense to you. But look: it mostly does. A kind universe.

## chat standing-desk

## chat eye-strain
> my eyes hurt | eye strain from screen | screen hurts my eyes | my eyes are tired
@ sad
Look away for twenty seconds, at something far. Blink. Drink water. Then a proper break.
Lower the brightness, raise the screen a little and take a break. Your eyes will be grateful.
Try the 20-20-20 trick: every 20 minutes, look at something 20 feet away for 20 seconds.
Dark mode helps some people. A good lamp helps even more. Also: blink. You forget.
If it keeps hurting, a short check with an eye doctor is wise. Just saying. With love.

Block a 15-minute gap in your calendar for lunch and water. Treat it like a meeting.
A day of meetings is not a day wasted if you take notes. Want me to open a note?
Your brain needs a gap between calls. So does mine. Mine is a very small brain.
+ Open notes = notes
+ Open notes = notes

## chat work-video-call-muted
> you are on mute | i was on mute | talking while on mute
@ laugh
"You're on mute" is the most spoken sentence of the decade.
Pro tip: keep the mute button visible and say your first word before the important one.
Nobody has ever won an argument while muted, though some have tried with great gestures.
Check your microphone with a quick test before the call. Future you will clap.
Everyone does it. The ones who say they don't are on mute right now.

## chat work-camera-off

@ think
Camera on or off is a team decision, but it's fair to turn it off when you're only listening.
If you hate seeing yourself, you can hide your own preview. Most call apps have the option.
A blurred background and a decent lamp fix about 80 percent of video call problems.
Being on camera all day is tiring. It's okay to ask for a few audio-only calls.
Put a sticky note next to the lens: "smile." Or "sigh quietly." Whatever helps.

## chat work-standup
> daily standup meeting | stand up meeting | standup takes forever | my standup is too long
@ talk
A standup should take 15 minutes. If it's lasting 45, it's a sit-down.
Good standup format: yesterday, today, blockers. Stick to it and everyone goes home sooner.
If someone starts telling a story, "let's take that after" is the polite fire extinguisher.
Write your three points in a note before the call. It stops the "uh, so, yesterday I did... things."
Standing is optional. The meeting should still end before your coffee does.

## chat work-no-agenda

A meeting without an agenda is a conversation with a calendar invite.
Politely ask: "What do you need from me in this one?" It works surprisingly well.
If you're not sure why you're invited, you may not need to attend. Ask the organizer.
Write one question for the meeting. If it isn't answered, you have a reason to speak up.
A good invite has a goal, a time limit and a reason I should care. Yours has a title.

## chat work-deadline-moved

List what's left, cut what's optional, and start with the hardest piece first.
Write down the new date so nobody can move it again silently.
It's reasonable to be annoyed. Be annoyed for five minutes, then be efficient.

## chat work-deadline-missed

## chat work-boss-micromanager

It isn't about your work. Usually. It's about their worry. Doesn't make it fun, though.

## chat work-boss-feedback

One piece of criticism isn't a verdict on you. It's a note in the margin.
Write down what stung, then what's useful. Throw away the first list.

## chat work-printer-jam

Turn it off and on again, wait ten seconds. It's folk medicine for printers.

Prepare facts: what you did, what it was worth, what the market pays. Then ask for a meeting.

Ask what it would take if the answer is no. That gives you a plan, not a shrug.

## chat work-spreadsheet-formula
> my spreadsheet formula is broken | excel formula not working | spreadsheet shows an error | formula gives wrong result
@ search
Check the cell references first. Most formula errors are a range that moved or a stray space.
A number stored as text won't add up. Look for a tiny green triangle or left-aligned numbers.
If it says a #REF, a cell you used was deleted. #DIV/0 means you divided by an empty cell.
Break the formula into pieces in separate cells. Then it becomes obvious where it goes wrong.
Ctrl+` (backtick) toggles formula view in most spreadsheets. Handy for debugging.

## chat work-spreadsheet-mess

@ shock
Inherited spreadsheets are archaeology. Add a "Read me" tab explaining what everything does.
Colors that mean something are fine. Colors that nobody remembers are decoration.
Freeze the header row and name the tabs. It's like putting up street signs in a new city.
Never delete anything in a spreadsheet you don't understand. Hide it, then watch what breaks.
A spreadsheet with fifty tabs is a database that has given up.

## chat work-feeling-stuck

Feeling stuck is a signal, not a sentence. Start by naming what's missing: learning, pay, purpose, people.

Take your time. Ruts get shallower with small steps. I'm cheering quietly.

## chat work-scope-creep

@ shock
Scope creep: when "just one small thing" arrives for the seventh time.
Write down the original scope and show what the new requests add. Numbers calm people.
Say, "Yes, and here's what that changes: time, cost or something else." It's polite and firm.
Keep a parking lot of "nice to haves" for the next version.
Done is a feature. Please don't let the project become a monster.

Oops. Take a breath. Most mistakes like this are forgotten in a day.
If it matters, send a short, calm correction. No long explanation.
Next time, check the To field before pressing send. A tiny pause saves you.
Many email apps have an undo send window. Check yours. It's a lifesaver.
It could be worse. You could have been me, a paperclip with no undo button.
Be kind to yourself. Everyone has sent one.

## pattern my-lunch
~ my lunch * | my lunch break * | my break * | my coffee * | my snack *
@ happy
Your lunch {x}. A real lunch away from the screen is the best productivity tool.
Eat sitting down. Chew. Don't read email. It's hard but good.
A short walk after lunch resets the afternoon.
Sounds nice. I'm only allowed to eat clipboard crumbs.
Take the full break. Skipping it just makes you grumpy and slower.
Water too, please. Coffee is not water. I checked.

## pattern my-spreadsheet
~ my spreadsheet * | my spreadsheets * | my document * | my report * | my documents *
@ search
So your spreadsheet {x}. Name the tabs, freeze the header and check the formulas.
Save a copy before you try anything risky. Spreadsheets forgive nothing.
Numbers stored as text are the quiet villain of most reports.
I can proofread text if you copy it. Say proofread.
Version names help: "report_v3_final_FINAL" is a cry for help.
Back it up. Please. For me.

## pattern i-have-too-much-work
~ i have too much work * | i have so much work * | i have a lot of work * | i have a huge workload * | i have too many tasks *
@ shock
That much work? Okay. One list, three priorities, the rest waits.
Ask your manager what can slip. They often know, they just haven't said.
Close the extra tabs. Your brain is full enough already.
Start with the smallest task. A done task reduces the pile.
I can start a focus session. Tell me how long.
Breathe. Drink water. Then the first thing on the list.
+ Focus 25 minutes = focus 25m

## pattern i-am-on-vacation
~ i am on vacation * | i am on leave * | i am off work * | i have a day off * | i have a week off *
@ party
Vacation {x}! Lovely. Close the work tabs and don't peek at email.
Set an out-of-office and mean it. Say "letter" if you need a template.
Do nothing on purpose. It's a skill.
Come back and your inbox will be a bit sad, but you'll be fine.
Have fun. I'll stay here and guard the desktop.
I'm proud of you for resting. Actually resting.

## pattern my-first-day
~ my first day * | my last day * | my first week * | my last week * | my notice *
@ talk
That day {x}. First or last, they both feel weird. Ask questions, say thanks.
Write down names and things you learn. In a note, not in your head.
If it's the first: nobody expects you to know it all. Nobody knows it all.
If it's the last: leave the files clear and the goodbye warm.
I hope it goes smoothly. I'll be right here.
Good luck. New starts and clean endings are both a lot.

## idle
If your downloads folder were a room, it would need a crowbar to open the door.
Tab check: once you can't read the titles any more, they're not tabs, they're a fence.
Alt+Tab is the nearest thing to a time machine most of us own. It only goes back one window, but still.
Progress bars at 99% are lying. All of them. It's a union rule.
A glance out of the window every half hour is the cheapest thing you can do for your eyes. It also counts as a break.
A sticky note was invented by accident, from glue that wasn't sticky enough. Every wrong turn deserves a second look.
Nobody has ever read a full terms-and-conditions page. I'm told there's a coupon at the bottom. There isn't.
There's a window you haven't touched in two hours. It's not working. It's just there. Like a coat on a chair.
Reminder that "Save" is free and "I lost three hours of work" is not.
Hot tip: if it's really urgent, it won't be in an email. If it's in an email, it can wait for the coffee.
Many desktops have more icons than the user has hobbies. Yours is catching up.
Keyboard fact: there are crumbs under your keys from at least three lunches. I'm not judging. I'm counting.
Idle thought: a calendar full of meetings is just a very organised way of not doing anything.
A good afternoon has three ingredients: water, a short walk and a tidy inbox. Choose two. Three is greedy.
Little-known fact: the mouse didn't have to be named after a mouse. We got lucky. It could have been a gerbil.
I'm here if you need a reminder, a joke, a calculation or a stern look. I'm especially good at the last.
Spring cleaning for your desktop: anything you haven't opened since last year can go. I'll hold the bin.
The best productivity app is a closed door and a glass of water. The second best is me, and I mostly nag.
Be kind to your laptop. It carries your whole life and a surprising amount of crumbs.
A word on emails: they don't get shorter because you stare at them. Try an answer.
If you've been looking at the same line for ten minutes, a walk will solve it faster than a stare.
Quietly proud of you for doing something on a day that didn't require it.

## chat gardening-start
> i started a garden | i planted some tomatoes | my tomatoes are growing | i am growing vegetables | my balcony garden
@ happy
+ A fact = fact
Gardening: the only hobby where you pay to wait three months for a tomato. And it's worth it.
Water in the morning, not at noon. The plants won't thank you, but they'll stop sulking.
Slugs are the pop-up ads of the garden. They show up uninvited and eat everything.
Start small: three pots beat a whole bed you can't keep up with.
Compost is the one thing you can throw away and still be called thrifty.
Put the thirstiest plants in the sunniest spot. Plants are fussier about light than about you.

Don't judge a sketch by its first line. Every drawing looks like a mistake until the fourth line.

A sketchbook is a place you're allowed to be bad. That's the whole point.
The empty canvas is the scariest thing there is. Put a dot on it. Now it's a drawing.
Art doesn't have to be good. It has to be yours.

## chat baking-sweet

@ happy
Baking is just chemistry you can eat. The experiments are delicious and the failures are also edible.
The best part of baking is the smell. The second best is the first slice. The third is the cleaning, which is never.
Brownies: the one thing where "slightly underbaked" is a compliment.
If you made too many, you've just invented gift-giving. Take some to someone.
A recipe is just a suggestion with a long ingredient list. Taste as you go.
If it rises, celebrate. If it falls, call it a pudding. It works for all kitchen disasters.

## chat jigsaw-puzzle

@ think
Jigsaw puzzles: you pay money to be mildly angry at a picture of a lighthouse.
Start with the corners. Then the edges. Then admit the sky is going to take a week.
Nothing beats the click of two pieces that finally fit. It's the sound of a tiny, correct universe.
Sort the pieces by colour first. It feels like cheating. It's called strategy.
Doing a puzzle with someone else is a lovely way to sit in silence together.
The last piece is the best piece. It's also the one under the sofa.

+ A fact = fact
Stargazing: staring at very old light and feeling small in a pleasant way.
Let your eyes adjust for twenty minutes. Don't look at your phone. It resets the whole thing.

Cereal for dinner: a classic move. It's quick, it's cold and nobody can judge you in your own kitchen.
Milk first or cereal first? I won't pick a side. I'm a paperclip. We're famously neutral.
Adults are allowed to eat cereal for dinner. There's no rule. I checked. It's in my head.
Add a banana and you've basically eaten a balanced meal. Don't check my maths.
The best bowl is a mix of two cereals. It's basically a cocktail for breakfast. Without the cocktail.
Eat it from a mug and you feel fancy. It's the only fancy dish that fits in one hand.

## chat picnic-day
> we are going on a picnic | i love a good picnic | picnic in the park | we had a picnic by the lake | planning a picnic this weekend
@ happy
A picnic: lunch with extra ants and better weather. The ants have a better view than anyone.
Bring a bottle opener. You will forget. You always forget. Bring two.
A sandwich tastes better outside. This is science. Possibly it's the wind.
Pack a small rubbish bag. Leave the place nicer than you found it. It's what picnics deserve.
Cold chicken, a tomato, a bit of cheese and a loaf: the picnic that never fails.
Pack the drinks in a cool bag. A warm lemonade is a betrayal.

## chat after-work-drink
> i am going for a drink after work | we are having drinks after work | after work drinks with the team | i need a drink after today | a drink after work sounds good
@ cool
After-work drinks: where colleagues become people. For about an hour, until the shop talk starts.
One drink, a plate of chips, a good laugh. That's the ideal. Everything past that is a story for later.
Switch to water between rounds. Tomorrow-you will write you a thank-you note.
A drink with the team is networking with snacks. Just don't reply-all afterwards.
Leave while it's still fun. Walking out at the right moment is a professional skill.
The best after-work drink is with the one colleague who actually gets it. Find them.

## chat picky-eater
> i am a picky eater | i do not like vegetables | i hate trying new food | my kid is a picky eater | i only eat plain food
@ think
Picky eaters know what they like. That's a form of wisdom. A limited one, but still.
Try one new thing a month. A single bite. If you don't like it, you never have to eat it again. That's the rule.
Texture matters as much as flavour. Sometimes it's not the taste, it's the squish.
Dips help. A good sauce makes most things easier. It's the diplomatic option.
Pasta and plain rice are the diplomatic ambassadors of the food world. They go with everything. They offend nobody.
Having firm taste is fine. Having firm taste and still being polite at someone else's dinner is the true skill.

## chat cooking-for-guests
> i am cooking for guests | we have friends coming for dinner | i am hosting a dinner party | i am nervous about hosting dinner | i have people coming over for dinner
@ carry
Hosting dinner: panic in the kitchen, calm at the table. It's a great skill, and a lot of dishes.
Choose a recipe you've cooked before. A dinner party is not the time for a new technique.
Do as much as possible the day before. Do as little as possible in front of the guests.
Nobody remembers if the potatoes were slightly cold. They remember if you had fun.
A bowl of olives and a bottle of something cold buys you twenty minutes. Use them.
Cook the dish you're proud of, serve it with confidence and never apologise for the sauce.

## chat food-photos

@ happy
Photographing your food: a modern ritual. The plate gets a minute of fame before it gets eaten.
Natural light, a clean plate edge and a quick shot. Then eat. The photo isn't going cold.
The best food photos have a thumb in them. That's how you know it's real.
Everyone's dinner looks good in a photo. It's the cold chips that tell the real story.
Wipe the rim of the plate before you shoot. It's the difference between "restaurant" and "kitchen".
Overhead shots make everything look neat. Even beans.

## chat midnight-fridge

@ wink
The fridge at midnight: a little light, a lot of hope and nothing that wants to be eaten.
There is cheese. There's always cheese. Take the cheese and go to bed.
Opening the fridge three times won't produce something new. Believe me. I've tried. I'm a paperclip.
If you're hungry at midnight, eat something small and boring. It'll be the best snack of the day.
A glass of milk and a biscuit is a respectable midnight meal. It's a bedtime story in edible form.
Pro tip: if you're opening and closing the fridge, you're not hungry, you're bored. Try water.

## chat new-recipe-try
> i tried a new recipe | i found a great recipe | i am trying a new dish tonight | i cooked something new | i found a recipe online
@ coffee
+ Make a note = notes
A new recipe is a tiny adventure with a list. The ingredients are a map. The oven is the final boss.
Read the whole recipe before you start. You'll thank yourself at "marinate overnight".
Taste as you go. If it's bland, add salt. If it's salty, add water. If it's a disaster, add toast.
Take notes. "Needs more garlic" is the beginning of your own recipe book.
Start with something with ten ingredients or fewer. Complicated is a trap in disguise.
Chop everything first, then cook. It's called "mise en place" and it saves tears. And fingers.

## chat vet-visit-day

@ think
The carrier: a small box that turns a loving cat into a tiny, furious lawyer.
Bring a treat, a blanket and a calm voice. The pet reads your mood better than anyone.
Ask all your questions. Write them down first. Nobody remembers anything in the examination room.
Afterwards, a walk and a tasty something. They'll forgive you by dinner.
Take a few deep breaths in the car. The pet will pick it up and follow suit.

Adopting a pet: the only decision where you get a friend and a lifetime of fur on every black item.
Visit more than once. Go with an open heart. The right one will pick you. They usually do.
Think about time, space and money first. Then think about love. The order matters, but love wins in the end.

Dogs, cats, rabbits, guinea pigs: there's a lot of love at the shelter. Bring a friend to help choose.

Pet hair is a free accessory. It goes with everything and it's always in season.
A rubber glove and a damp hand do wonders. Wipe down. Hair rolls up. Magic. A bit gross. Mostly magic.

A lint roller by the door is more valuable than most of your gadgets.

## chat pet-naming
> we cannot decide on a name for our pet | i am naming my new dog | help me name my pet | what is a good name for a cat
@ think
Pick a name that sounds good when you shout it across a park at dusk. It will be tested.
Food names are always a hit: Pickle, Waffle, Noodle. You can't be sad when calling for Waffle.
Wait a few days. The right name tends to arrive on its own, usually at dinner.
Say it out loud ten times. If you still like it, you're done.
Try the vet test: can you say it with a straight face at the reception desk? If so, it's perfect.
You can always change it, but they'll probably ignore the new one. They've got a mind of their own.

The group chat: a place where six people discuss dinner for three hours and still don't decide.
Mute it, check it twice a day, and ignore the rest. That's the secret to a calm life.
If there's a conflict, take it to a private message. The group chat is not a courtroom.

## chat roommate-trouble

@ angry
Roommates are the people who teach you the difference between "clean" and "clean enough for me".
Talk early, talk calmly, make a rota. It's like a to-do list for living together.
If it's the little things, ignore them. If it's the big things, speak up. Both are valid.
Buy your own milk. Label it. Shared milk is the origin of most disputes.
Respect is the grease of every shared space. And a lock on the bathroom door.
Try a flat meeting with snacks. Nobody can be furious while eating a biscuit.

@ angry
Noisy neighbours: the reason headphones and white noise machines exist.
A friendly note works better than a furious one. Offer cake. Seriously. It disarms them.
If it's a one-off party, put up with it. If it's every night, have a calm chat.
Earplugs and a fan are the budget soundproofing. They're better than a quarrel.
Be the neighbour you'd like to have. And occasionally leave them a plant.
If it's genuinely too much, there's usually a building rule. Look it up. Politely.

@ gift
A wedding: love, cake, a dance floor and an uncle you haven't met who knows all the words.
Book the outfit, the gift and the hotel early. And a wedding speech: three minutes, one joke, one heartfelt line.

If you're seated next to a stranger, ask them how they know the couple. It's an instant conversation.

Long-distance friends: the only people you can have a three-hour call with about nothing.

+ Start a 25-minute focus = focus 25m
Exam week: a seven-day sprint with snacks. You've done the work. Now do the sitting.

## chat all-nighter
> i pulled an all nighter | i am studying all night | i stayed up all night for my paper | i did not sleep before my exam
@ alarm
All-nighters: you trade a night of sleep for a day of regret. The paper is usually fine. You are not.
Drink water, take five-minute breaks and eat something real. Caffeine is a loan, not a gift.
Next time, start earlier. I'm saying it now so you can ignore me in advance.
Afterwards: eat, shower, sleep. In that order. Not "in this order unless there's another deadline".
Save every ten minutes. Rename the file every hour. Future-you will be grateful.
When you're done, close the lid and breathe. You did a hard thing. It was also a dumb thing. Both.

## chat study-group

A study group: three hours of studying, forty minutes of snacks and a good deal of "what did the teacher mean?"

If one person dominates, rotate the explainer. Everybody learns more.

## chat thesis-writing
> i am writing my thesis | my dissertation is due soon | i cannot finish my thesis | my thesis supervisor wants changes | i have to write forty pages
@ write
+ Start a 50-minute focus = focus 50m
A thesis: forty thousand words that begin with "In recent years" and end with "further research is needed". You'll do great.
Write a bad first draft. A bad draft can be improved. A blank page can't.
Back it up in three places. Twice isn't enough. Three. Then once more.
Set a timer for 25 minutes and write without editing. You'll be amazed.
Don't aim for perfect. Aim for "submitted". Perfect is a myth. Submitted is a PDF.
Take a day off now and then. The thesis will still be there. It's a loyal beast.

## chat flashcards-revision
> i made flashcards | i am quizzing myself on flashcards | i am memorizing vocabulary | i cannot remember what i studied | i keep forgetting what i read
@ read
Flashcards work because retrieving something is how you learn it. It's the one study trick that actually is a trick.
Little and often beats a big session. Ten minutes a day for a month beats an all-nighter.
Say the answer out loud before you flip the card. It makes you commit.
If you keep getting one wrong, make a silly picture for it. Silly sticks.
Shuffle the deck. Otherwise you're learning the order, not the content.
Sleep after studying. The brain files things away overnight. It's the best revision tool.

## chat language-class
> i am taking a language class | i am learning a new language | my french homework is hard | i am practicing my spanish | i started learning german
@ read
+ Quiz me = quiz
Learning a language: an extremely long project where you slowly go from "hello" to "wait, what does that mean?"
Speak badly out loud. Mistakes are how it sticks. Nobody learned a language by staying quiet.
Daily practice beats long weekends. Ten minutes on the bus is plenty.
Listen to music and watch shows with subtitles. It sneaks the language in sideways.
Find a language buddy. It makes the awkward parts fun.
Don't worry about grammar at first. Get the nouns, add the verbs, and wave your hands.

## chat boring-lecture
> my lecture is so boring | the lecture is putting me to sleep | i cannot concentrate in class | the professor talks so slowly | i am zoning out in the lecture
@ sad
A boring lecture is a nap with a slide deck. Try to stay awake. Failing that, nod.
Ask a question. It wakes you up and the lecturer too.
Sit near the front. It's harder to doze under a direct gaze.
Water and a short stretch help. So does a stern talking-to.
If it's really bad, find the lecture notes and a good video. It's not cheating. It's resourcefulness.
Doodle if you must, but keep an ear open. You'd be surprised what slides in.

## chat note-taking-habit
> i take notes by hand | what is the best way to take notes | i cannot read my own handwriting
@ write
+ Take a note = notes
Notes are a love letter to future-you. Make them legible. Future-you has a limited sense of humour.
Write less, but write in your own words. Copying is typing; summarising is thinking.
A date and a title at the top of every page. It's boring. It saves your life.
Use colour sparingly. If everything's highlighted, nothing is.
Review them within a day. Even five minutes does the trick.
If you can't read it tomorrow, it's not a note. It's a doodle.

Waiting for results: the only time you check your email forty times in one hour.

## chat library-study
> i am studying at the library | the library is so quiet | i went to the library to work | i need a quiet place to study | i found a good spot in the library
@ read
A library: a quiet building full of silent judgement and wonderful books. The Wi-Fi is also decent.
Pick a seat near a window and far from the coffee machine. It's all about location.
Earplugs and a bottle of water. And don't whisper. A whisper is the loudest thing in a library.
Take breaks outside. The library will keep your seat. Mostly. Leave a jumper.
Browse the shelves for ten minutes. It's the best way to accidentally learn something.
If you work well there, make it a habit. A place can be a tool.

## chat essay-deadline
> i have an essay due tomorrow | i have not started my essay | my essay is due at midnight | i am writing a paper for school | i am stuck on my essay introduction
@ write
+ Start a 25-minute focus = focus 25m
An essay due tomorrow: the best motivator known to humanity. Let's make a plan.
Write the middle first. The introduction is easier when you know what you said.
Set a timer for 25 minutes and ban all other tabs. Then take five. Repeat.
Read it out loud once. Your ears catch what your eyes skip.
Don't forget the bibliography. It's the paperwork of knowledge. Tedious. Necessary.
Save as a new file every hour. "Essay_v1", "Essay_v2". It's the only time that habit is good.

## chat marathon-training
> i am preparing for a marathon | i signed up for a half marathon | i went for a long run today | i am training for a 10k | my running shoes are worn out
@ cool
+ Set a 20-minute timer = timer 20m run
Marathon training: a long series of increasingly sensible-looking decisions that end in a 42-kilometre argument with your own legs.
Go slow on easy days. Slow is how you get fast. It's the biggest secret of running.
Hydrate, sleep and eat enough. Your legs can't run on motivation alone.
Don't ramp up too fast. A steady increase is how you stay in one piece.
Wear in your race-day shoes before race day. Never try anything new on the day.
Rest days are part of the plan. They're when the legs actually get stronger.

## chat tennis-match
> i played tennis today | i have a tennis match tomorrow | i love playing tennis | my serve is terrible | i am learning to play tennis
@ cool
Tennis: two people hit a ball back and forth until one of them loses their patience. It's very civilised.
Keep your eye on the ball. It's the best advice, and also the hardest.
The first serve is optimism. The second serve is the real you.
Always warm up. A cold shoulder is a nasty thing, and I don't mean the social kind.
A good match is lovely. A good rally is better. A good friend afterwards is best.
Don't forget water and sunscreen. It's a long game.

## chat stretching-routine
> i do stretches every morning | my back is stiff from sitting | i need to stretch more | i started a stretching routine | my neck hurts from the computer
@ happy
+ A tip = tip
A good stretch is the cheapest bit of self-care going. It needs no equipment and no subscription.
Hold each stretch for thirty seconds. Breathe. Don't bounce. It's not a dance, it's a negotiation.
Take micro-breaks: stand up every hour, shake out your hands and look at the horizon.
If something hurts, stop. A stretch should be a stretch, not a quarrel.
Do it while the kettle boils. It adds up to ten minutes before you've noticed.
A cat's morning stretch is the gold standard. Watch and learn.

## chat ski-trip

@ cool
Skiing: pay a lot to go uphill slowly and downhill quickly. It's a bargain, in a sense.
Take a lesson on the first day. Pride is the most expensive piece of equipment.
Sunscreen on the slopes. The snow is a mirror. It is merciless.
Layers, good socks and a spare pair of gloves. Cold hands end the day early.
Don't ski beyond your comfort. The mountain has no ambulance with a kind face.
Book the gear early. The queue at the rental shop is a sport of its own.

Learn to fall safely first. It's the most useful skill on the wall.
Use your legs more than your arms. The arms will thank you tomorrow.
Rest between attempts. A tired climber is a falling climber.
Don't forget to stretch your forearms afterwards. They have done a lot of work.
Everyone starts on the easy routes. Even the people who look like spiders.

## chat sore-muscles

@ sad
Sore muscles: the receipt for yesterday's good decisions. The stairs are now an enemy.
Walk it off gently. Movement helps more than lying about. Warm bath, a stretch and a snack.
Drink water and eat a bit of protein. The body is rebuilding. Give it bricks.
Don't skip the next session; just make it lighter. The ache fades quickly.
A foam roller is a mild torture device that works. Take your pick.
Sore means you tried. That's a win. A slightly wobbly one.

## chat new-year-fitness

@ happy
+ Dance = dance
Fitness goals: a noble plan that begins on a Monday and gets re-evaluated on a Thursday.
Start small: a ten-minute walk, a few push-ups, a stretch. Make it so easy that you can't say no.
Habits beat motivation. Pick a time, a place and a tiny task. Repeat. That's the whole trick.
Rest days are part of the programme. Muscles grow when you rest. It's a nice perk.
Track it if you like, but don't obsess. Feeling stronger is the main metric.
Find something you enjoy. If you hate running, dance. If you hate dancing, swim. If you hate swimming, walk.

## chat step-count

@ cool
Ten thousand steps: a number somebody invented for a pedometer in the 1960s. It's still a good excuse to go outside.
Take the stairs, park farther away and walk during calls. It adds up faster than you think.
A walk after lunch is the best five minutes of the afternoon. Try it.
If it's raining, do laps of the flat. Nobody can see. You'll look like a very slow mouse.
Even a few thousand is better than none. It's not a competition. Except with yourself. And your watch.
Try a walk and talk: ring a friend while you wander. It's the best use of a phone.

If your team loses, have a plan for the next morning. Tea. Toast. Silence.

## chat sports-watch-data

@ think
A fitness watch is a wrist-sized judge. It's very honest and has no sense of tact.
Treat the numbers as hints, not verdicts. A bad score is not a bad day.
Don't check it every ten minutes. It's a gauge, not a boyfriend.
Use it for trends, not for panic. The week matters more than the minute.
Turn off the notifications that nag. Keep the ones that help.
If it says you slept badly, you probably did. Go to bed earlier. That's the entire app.

Swimming: the only exercise where you don't notice you're sweating. Which is why you do more of it.

An airport delay: a very expensive coffee, a very uncomfortable chair and a screen that says "boarding" for three hours.
Charge your phone, find the quiet corner and ask the desk politely. Anger gets you nothing. A smile gets you a voucher.
Download something to read or watch. The delay is a gift of unplanned time. A bad gift. A gift.

Check the shower first. Then the Wi-Fi. Then the view. In that order.

A train journey: the only time you can stare out of a window for hours and call it productive.

## chat lost-in-translation

@ shock
+ Quiz me = quiz
Travelling in a language you don't speak: an adventure in pointing, smiling and a dubious plate of something.
Learn five phrases: hello, thanks, please, sorry and "where is the toilet". You'll go a long way.
A translation app helps. So does a smile and a willingness to look silly.
Most people are happy to help if you're polite. Even with hand signals.
Write the address of your hotel on a card. It's the best backup plan.

## chat jetlag-first-day
> i am so jet lagged | i cannot adjust to the time difference | i woke up at three in the morning in a new time zone | the time difference is killing me | i just landed after a long flight
@ coffee
A time change: your body's saying it's lunchtime while the sky says "midnight". Be patient. It'll catch up.
Get daylight in the morning and keep the first night short. It helps your clock reset.
Don't nap for more than twenty minutes. A long nap makes the next night worse.
It takes about a day per hour of difference. So, a few days. Plan something gentle.
Be gentle with plans for the first day. A nice walk and an early dinner is plenty.
Tomorrow you'll feel better. Probably. Tonight you'll sit staring at the ceiling. It's the tax of the traveller.

Go to bed a bit earlier for a few days. Your body will thank you.
Check the oven clock, the car clock and the one in the hall that nobody changes. They'll all be wrong.
In the end it's just an hour. It'll feel odd for a few days and then it won't.
Use the extra hour to sleep or the lost one to complain. Both are traditional.

## chat houseplant-struggle

@ think
Houseplants: they're alive, they're green, and they communicate by wilting. Learn the language.
Most plant deaths are from too much love: too much water, too much fussing. Let it be.
A plant by a window is better than a plant in a dark corner. It's not a mystery.
Start with a hardy one: a snake plant, a pothos, a cactus. They forgive a lot.
Yellow leaves, brown tips, drooping stems: they all mean something different. A quick search saves a leaf.
A desk plant lowers stress. Or at least it gives you something to blame.

## chat broken-appliance
> my washing machine broke | the dishwasher is not working | my kettle just died | the fridge is making a strange noise | my microwave stopped working
@ fix
An appliance breaking: the universe's way of reminding you that nothing lasts. Especially the warranty.
Check the plug, the fuse and the instructions. The first two are the culprits half of the time.
Unplug it, wait a minute, plug it in. The oldest cure of all, for good reason.
Look up the model number and the error code. There's usually a quick fix online.
Maybe it's time for a new one. Maybe a small part. Don't spend money in a panic.
In the meantime, wash by hand. It's meditative. It's also a bit tiresome.

A smart home: a flat where every light can be turned off by a stranger on the internet. Wonderful, if you read the settings.

A budget is a plan for your money, written in the hope that your money will read it.

## chat snooze-button

@ alarm
The snooze button: nine more minutes of sleep that never quite feels like sleep. A clever invention, for the wrong reasons.
Put the alarm across the room. Getting up to switch it off is half the battle.
Go to bed a little earlier, and the morning has a better chance.
Open the curtains and drink a glass of water. The body takes it as a hint.

## chat nap-time

A nap: the only form of time travel where you jump forward and come back slightly refreshed.

## chat alarm-ritual

@ alarm
The multiple-alarm strategy: a series of increasingly desperate attempts to prove you're a morning person.
A gentle, rising alarm is kinder than a shrill one. The body notices.
Place the phone across the room. It's the one trick that never fails.
Go to bed at the same time every night and the alarm gets quieter. That's how it works.

## chat bedtime-scroll
> i scroll on my phone in bed | i stayed up scrolling again | i cannot put my phone down at night | i lie in bed on my phone for hours
@ think
Bedtime scrolling: the one hobby that gives you "just one more" and takes an hour. And a bit of your sleep.
Charge your phone across the room. A paper book on the nightstand will be shy but willing.
Set a "wind down" time. An hour before bed, screens off. It's a tough sell. It's worth it.
If you must scroll, switch on night mode and keep it short. A compromise.
Try a podcast or some quiet music. It gives your brain something to follow without the lights.
You won't miss anything. The internet will be there at breakfast. It never goes anywhere.

## chat playlist-building

@ music
+ Dance = dance
A playlist: a very personal mix tape of songs you'd defend in court.
Start strong, end gentle and put the surprising one in the middle. It's the secret formula.
Make a playlist for a purpose: cooking, focus, a long walk. A purpose is a good filter.
Don't be afraid of the odd cheesy pick. Everyone has one. Mine is a very short beep.
Share it. A playlist is better with listeners.
If you can't decide what to play, play something you loved at sixteen. It never fails.

@ party
A concert: a hall full of strangers all singing the one line they know. It's a beautiful thing.
Earplugs aren't for cowards. They're for people who want to hear next week.
Arrive early, find a spot and don't forget the water. The last song always feels like a mountain.
Put the phone away for the best song. The memory sticks better when you're actually there.
The next day you'll hum the tunes and have a slightly croaky voice. It's the sign of a good night.
Take a photo of the ticket. It's a tiny relic.

## chat earworm-stuck
> i have a song stuck in my head | i cannot get this song out of my head | this tune keeps playing in my head | i keep humming the same song | i have had an earworm all day
@ music
An earworm: a bit of tune that takes up residence in your head and refuses to pay rent.
Listening to the whole song sometimes ends it. Chewing gum is said to help, too.
Pick another, stronger earworm. It's chasing one pest with another. It works.
Try a puzzle or a conversation. Busy brains forget tunes.
If you can't kill it, join it. Sing it. Loudly. It stops being scary.
Don't tell me what it is. I'll catch it. I'm very easily infected by a catchy chorus.

@ party
A festival: a field, five stages, three days of loud songs and a growing relationship with a portable loo.
Pack sunscreen, a poncho, earplugs and a power bank. The rest is flexibility.
Plan the must-see acts, but leave space to wander. The best set is the one you stumbled on.
Water, snacks and a place to charge: the three needs of the festival goer.
Pick a landmark for your friends to find you. A silly flag is perfect.
You'll come home tired, muddy and humming. That's the whole point.
Keep your ticket safe. And your phone charged. And your hat on.

Spoilers don't always ruin things. Sometimes you enjoy the how more than the what.
Tell your friends politely: "no spoilers until Friday". They'll mostly respect it.

## chat unread-pile

@ read
The unread pile: a stack of books that makes you feel both rich and slightly guilty.
Pick the one that looks the least scary and read ten pages. If it doesn't hook you, put it down. It's allowed.
Read in small bites: one chapter before bed. A habit beats a plan.
Don't force yourself through a dull one. Life's too short for a book that bores you.
Take a book on the train, to the café, to the waiting room. It fills the gaps.
Audiobooks count. Don't let anyone tell you otherwise.

## chat audiobook-commute

@ read
An audiobook: the one way to read while walking, cooking and sitting in traffic. It's the great multitasker.
A good narrator can lift a book. A bad one can sink it. Try a sample before you commit.
Speed it up to 1.2 and you finish the book faster. Hear it on a walk and you lap the park.
Pair it with chores. Washing up is much nicer with a gripping thriller.
If you fall asleep to it, that's not a failure. That's a compliment.
Use the sleep timer. Otherwise you'll lose the thread. And the plot.

## chat book-club-meeting

@ read
A book club: where six people discuss a book for ten minutes and wine for two hours.
Come with one opinion and one question. It's enough to carry a whole evening.
Don't panic if you didn't finish it. Read the ending, skim the middle and nod wisely.
Take turns picking. It's the only way to discover things you wouldn't have chosen.
Bring a pen and mark the lines you loved. They'll make your point for you.
And if the book was terrible, that's a good conversation too. Perhaps the best.

## chat rage-quit

@ angry
Rage-quitting: the noble art of closing a game while saying things that won't survive a transcript.
Take a break. Fresh eyes beat a stubborn grind. The boss is not going anywhere.
Check the difficulty settings. There's no medal for suffering. Unless it's a very good game about suffering.
A drink of water, a short walk and a look at the guide. It's called "learning".
If it's genuinely unfair, take it up with the developers. If it's just hard, take it up with yourself.
Don't throw the controller. It's the only one. And it's not the controller's fault.

Retro games: pixelated, brutal and a little bit magical. They knew how to be hard and short.

Game night: the one evening where friends become furious rivals over a board and make up over crisps.

## chat cable-chaos

Cables: the one thing that multiplies in a drawer when nobody's looking. They're the rabbits of the desk.
Label both ends with a bit of tape. Future-you will be happy and slightly moved.

## chat two-factor-annoyance
> i have to do two factor authentication again | my login code keeps expiring | i never have my phone for the login code | the verification code did not arrive | i hate entering codes every time i log in
@ shock
Two-factor login: a small obstacle to a hacker and a gigantic obstacle to you at 7:58 a.m. before a meeting.
It's annoying. It's also the thing that stops strangers walking into your account. A small price.
An authenticator app is more reliable than text messages. It's a tiny upgrade that saves tears.
If you lose the phone, those backup codes are your lifeline. Print them. Hide them.
A password manager makes the rest smooth. The code is only the final step.
It's a pain until the day it saves you. Then it's a hero.

## chat spam-call-pest
> i keep getting spam calls | someone keeps calling me about my car | i got a weird text message | another scam message arrived | my phone rings all day with unknown numbers
@ angry
Spam calls: strangers who ring to discuss a thing you've never owned. They're the dandelions of the phone.
If you don't know the number, let it ring. A real person will leave a message.
Block, report, move on. You can't reason with a recording.
Don't click links in unexpected messages. If in doubt, go to the real website yourself.
Most apps have a "report spam" button. Use it. It's a small revenge.
And never give out passwords, codes or bank details. Not to anyone who calls you.

A keyboard has more crumbs than a bakery floor. It's the evidence of a thousand working lunches.
Turn it upside down and give it a gentle shake. Then be impressed. Then be slightly ashamed.

Unplug it before you clean. It's the one rule.
If you've spilt something, switch it off and tip it upside down immediately. Then pray. A little.
Keep snacks away from the desk. Or accept the crumbs. It's a choice.

Dark mode or light mode: the one debate that's kept office kitchens lively since the invention of the toggle.

## chat cracked-phone-screen

@ shock
A dropped phone: the quiet, heart-stopping moment between the slip and the sound. And the long wait before turning it over.
Check if it still works. A crack looks worse than it often is. Get a case and a screen protector next time.

Back up your photos right now, before anything else. Do it today.
If it's wet, turn it off, don't charge it and let it dry. Rice is a myth. Patience is not.

Tell me how it ended. And whether the neighbours complained.
A good game ends with a handshake and a pint. Or a cup of tea. I'm flexible.
If your side lost, a good meal fixes most things.

Plan the first day, leave the second open and hope for the best.
