## chat know-black-holes
> black holes | event horizon | inside a black hole | black hole gravity
@ think
+ Another fact = fact
A black hole is a place where gravity is so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape once it crosses the event horizon.
Many black holes form when a very massive star runs out of fuel and collapses. Not a rule for every star, though. Most just fade.
The first picture of a black hole's shadow was released in 2019. It looked like a glowing orange donut.
At the center of our galaxy sits a supermassive black hole with roughly four million times the mass of the Sun.
Black holes don't hoover up everything nearby. From a distance, their gravity acts like that of any object with the same mass.

## chat know-moon-far-side
> far side of the moon | moon facing earth | tidally locked moon | moon always shows
@ read
We always see the same side of the Moon because it spins exactly once per orbit. That's called being tidally locked.
The far side of the Moon isn't "dark". It gets just as much sunlight as the side we see. We simply never see it from Earth.
The Moon's gravity is only about one sixth of Earth's, which is why those lunar jumps looked so floaty.
The Moon has quakes. Seismometers left by the Apollo missions recorded them for years.
Twelve people have walked on the Moon, all between 1969 and 1972.

## chat know-mars-rovers
> mars rovers | red planet mars | martian day | mars rover landing
@ search
Mars looks red because its dust contains a lot of iron oxide. That's rust, basically. A very old, very dry planet.
A Martian day, called a sol, lasts about 24 hours and 40 minutes. A very mild jet lag.
Gravity on Mars is only about 38 percent of Earth's. You'd weigh a lot less, and so would your luggage.
The rover Perseverance landed on Mars in February 2021, and it's been collecting rock samples ever since.
In 2021 the little helicopter Ingenuity made the first powered flight on another planet. It flew for about 39 seconds.

## chat know-sun-core
> the sun's core | how hot is the sun | sun fusion energy | solar wind
@ think
The Sun is mostly hydrogen and helium. In its core, hydrogen nuclei fuse into helium, and that releases the energy that reaches us.
The Sun's core is about 15 million degrees Celsius. The surface is only around 5,500, which is the cooler bit.
Sunspots are patches on the Sun's surface that are a little cooler than their surroundings, which is why they look dark.
The Sun constantly streams out charged particles called the solar wind. Some of them make the northern and southern lights.
The Sun loses millions of tonnes of mass every second, turning it into energy. It has plenty left.

## chat know-saturn-rings
> saturn rings | rings of saturn | how saturn got rings | ice in saturn rings
@ read
Saturn's rings are made of countless pieces of ice and rock, from grains of dust to chunks as big as houses.
The rings are enormous across but incredibly thin, often only about ten meters thick in places.
The rings may be relatively young, perhaps only a few hundred million years old. Scientists are still debating it.
Galileo first spotted Saturn's rings in 1610, but his telescope was too weak, so he thought the planet had "ears".
Saturn is a gas giant with no solid surface, and it has well over a hundred known moons.

## chat know-milky-way
> milky way galaxy | our galaxy | spiral galaxy | galactic center
@ think
The Milky Way is a spiral galaxy about 100,000 light-years across, with a bar of stars through its middle.
Our Sun sits about 26,000 light-years from the galactic center, out in one of the spiral arms.
The Sun orbits the center of the Milky Way once every 225 to 250 million years or so. The last time we were here, dinosaurs were just starting out.
When you see the Milky Way as a pale band across a dark sky, you're looking edge-on into the disk of our own galaxy.
Our galaxy has hundreds of billions of stars, and astronomers think most of them have at least one planet.

## chat know-comets
> comets tails | halley's comet | comet nucleus | dirty snowball
@ spin
A comet is a lump of ice, dust and rock. When it nears the Sun, heat releases gas and dust, and that makes its glowing tail.
A comet's tail always points away from the Sun, even when the comet is heading back out. The solar wind pushes it.
Halley's Comet comes back roughly every 76 years. It's next due in 2061, so there's time to find a good deckchair.
The solid part of a comet, the nucleus, is often only a few kilometers wide, yet its tail can stretch for millions of kilometers.
Most comets live in the far, cold edges of the solar system and only visit us once in a very long while.

## chat know-eclipses
> solar eclipse | total eclipse | lunar eclipse | eclipse glasses
@ read
In a solar eclipse, the Moon passes between the Sun and Earth and blocks the Sun's light along a narrow strip of land.
Totality, when the Sun is completely covered, lasts at most about seven and a half minutes. Usually far less.
Never look at an eclipse without proper eclipse glasses. Even a partial one can damage your eyes.
In a lunar eclipse, Earth's shadow falls across the Moon. It often turns a dull red, because some sunlight bends through our atmosphere.
A total solar eclipse is possible because the Moon is about 400 times smaller than the Sun and about 400 times closer.

## chat know-life-on-iss
> life on the iss | astronauts sleeping | living in space | astronauts exercise
@ read
Astronauts on the International Space Station see about 16 sunrises and 16 sunsets every day, because they orbit Earth every 90 minutes.
There's no up or down in orbit, so astronauts sleep in sleeping bags tied to the wall. Some sleep in little cabins.
Astronauts exercise about two hours a day, to keep their muscles and bones from weakening in weightlessness.
Most of the water on the station is recycled, including moisture from the air. Every drop gets a lot of use.
The space station has been continuously occupied since November 2000. That's a very long housewarming.

## chat know-star-colours
> why stars twinkle | star colours | brightest star | blue and red stars
@ search
Stars twinkle because their light passes through Earth's moving, bumpy atmosphere. Planets, which look like tiny discs, twinkle far less.
The color of a star shows its temperature. Blue stars are the hottest, white and yellow ones are in the middle, and red ones are the coolest.
Seen from Earth, the brightest star in the night sky is Sirius, in the constellation Canis Major.
When you look at a star, you see light that left it years ago. Sometimes thousands of years ago. You're looking at the past.
Our Sun is a yellow-white star, quite ordinary in size, among hundreds of billions in our galaxy.

## chat know-octopus-smarts
> octopus intelligence | octopus arms | octopus camouflage | smart octopuses
@ think
An octopus has most of its neurons in its arms, so each arm can do some thinking and tasting on its own.
Octopuses change colour and even skin texture in a fraction of a second, using tiny pigment sacs called chromatophores.
With no bones, an octopus can squeeze through any gap bigger than its beak, which is the only hard part of its body.
Some octopuses collect coconut shells and carry them around to use as portable shelters. Tool use, in a very small way.
Most octopuses live only one to a few years, which makes their cleverness all the more remarkable.

## chat know-honey-bees
> honey bee hive | queen bee | how bees make honey | bee colony
@ read
A honey bee colony has one queen, thousands of workers and, in summer, a few hundred male drones.
In her whole lifetime, a single worker bee makes only about one twelfth of a teaspoon of honey.
Bees can see ultraviolet light, which shows them patterns on flowers that we can't see at all.
A honey bee beats its wings about 200 times a second. That's what makes the buzz.
To make honey, bees collect nectar, pass it between each other, and fan it with their wings until the water evaporates.

## chat know-dolphin-whistles
> dolphin whistles | dolphin echolocation | dolphin names | how dolphins talk
@ happy
Each bottlenose dolphin develops its own signature whistle, which works a bit like a name. Others use it to call it.
Dolphins use echolocation: they send out clicks and listen to the echoes to see shapes, even in murky water.
Dolphins live in groups called pods, and their babies are called calves. They stay close to their mothers for years.
A dolphin sleeps with half its brain at a time, so that it can keep surfacing for air.
Dolphins are mammals, not fish. They breathe air through a blowhole on top of their heads.

## chat know-elephants
> elephant trunk | african elephants | elephant herd | elephant ears
@ happy
An elephant's trunk has tens of thousands of muscle units and no bones. It can pick up a peanut and also lift a log.
African elephants are the largest land animals, with big bulls weighing as much as six tonnes.
Elephants talk to each other with deep rumbles, some too low for humans to hear, that can travel for kilometers.
Those big ears aren't just for show. They're full of blood vessels and help the elephant shed heat.
Elephant herds are led by an older female, the matriarch, who remembers where the water and food are.

## chat know-owls-hunt
> how owls hunt | silent owls | owl pellets | owl eyes
@ search
Owls fly almost silently, thanks to soft, fringed feathers that break up the noise of air flowing over their wings.
The flat, dish-shaped face of an owl works like a satellite dish and funnels sounds to its ears.
Owls swallow their prey whole and later cough up a neat pellet of bones and fur. Scientists study them to see what owls eat.
Many owls hunt at night and can find a mouse under snow just by hearing it.
An owl's eyes are fixed in their sockets, so it can't look sideways. It has to turn its whole head instead.

## chat know-sharks-science
> shark teeth | whale shark | shark skeleton | sharks cartilage
@ read
A shark's skeleton is made of cartilage, the same flexible stuff as your ears and nose, not bone.
Sharks keep growing new teeth all their lives. Some lose and replace thousands of them.
Sharks have existed for hundreds of millions of years, long before the first dinosaurs.
The biggest fish in the sea, the whale shark, is a gentle giant that filters tiny plankton from the water.
Sharks can sense the faint electric fields of other animals using special pores around their snouts.

## chat know-ant-colonies
> ant colony | leafcutter ants | ant trails | queen ant
@ search
An ant colony can hold millions of ants, all working together without a boss telling them what to do.
Ants leave chemical trails called pheromones, so their sisters can follow the shortest path to food.
Leafcutter ants don't eat leaves. They carry them home to grow a fungus, which they farm and eat.
An ant queen can live for many years, laying eggs for most of that time. Some queens reach almost thirty.
Ants don't have lungs. They breathe through tiny holes along their bodies.

## chat know-crow-brains
> crow intelligence | crows tools | smart crows | ravens and crows
@ think
New Caledonian crows make tools from twigs and leaves, and they can bend wire into a hook to fish out food.
In lab puzzles inspired by an old fable, crows drop stones into a tube to raise the water level and reach a treat.
Crows can recognize human faces and remember who treated them badly, then teach their friends.
Ravens are usually larger than crows, with a heavier beak and a deeper, croaky voice.
Crows can learn to count small numbers and solve puzzles that stump young children.

## chat know-bat-echo
> bat echolocation | fruit bats | bat colony | how bats see
@ search
Many bats find their way and their food in the dark by calling out and listening to the echoes. That's echolocation.
About one in five mammal species is a bat. There are over 1,400 kinds, from tiny to giant.
Fruit bats help plants by spreading seeds and pollinating flowers, so many forests depend on them.
A cave in Texas holds a colony of millions of Mexican free-tailed bats. At dusk they pour out in a huge, swirling cloud.
Bats aren't blind. Many see quite well, and some of the big fruit bats rely mostly on sight and smell.

## chat know-blue-whale
> blue whale | baleen whales | whale blowhole | whales evolved
@ read
The blue whale is the largest animal ever known to have lived. It can reach around 30 meters long.
Baleen whales don't have teeth. They filter huge mouthfuls of water through plates of baleen to catch krill.
A whale breathes through a blowhole on top of its head, and a big exhale can shoot a spout several meters high.
Whales descended from land mammals that returned to the sea about 50 million years ago. Their closest living relatives are hippos.
Whale songs can travel enormous distances underwater, and humpbacks sing songs that change over the years.

## chat know-penguin-life
> emperor penguins | penguin colony | penguins swim | galapagos penguin
@ happy
Penguins are birds that can't fly, but their wings work as flippers, and they "fly" through the water.
Male emperor penguins keep the egg warm on their feet through the Antarctic winter while the females are out feeding.
A penguin's black back and white belly is camouflage. From above it blends with dark water, from below with the bright surface.
Most penguins live in the Southern Hemisphere, and one species lives at the equator, on the Galápagos Islands.
Penguins huddle in big groups and take turns standing on the cold outside edge to share the warmth.

## chat know-sloth-life
> sloths move | slow sloths | sloth fur | how sloths live
@ spin
Sloths move slowly on purpose. Their leafy diet is low in energy, so slowness saves them fuel.
A sloth usually comes down from its tree only about once a week, and the trip is mostly for bathroom reasons.
Algae can grow in a sloth's fur, giving it a slightly green tint that helps it blend in with the leaves.
Sloths are surprisingly good swimmers, despite how they look on land.
A sloth's stomach has several chambers, and it can take days for it to digest a single leafy meal.

## chat know-heart-works
> how the heart works | heartbeat sound | heart valves | pumping blood
@ read
Your heart is a muscle about the size of your fist, with four chambers: two small ones on top and two strong ones below.
The "lub-dub" of a heartbeat is the sound of heart valves snapping shut, one set after the other.
At rest, your heart pumps roughly five liters of blood a minute. That's about everything you've got, once a minute.
The heart has its own electrical pacemaker, a tiny patch of cells that sets the rhythm. Your brain only turns it up or down.
Every heartbeat sends blood through a network of vessels that reaches nearly every part of the body.

## chat know-brain-wonder
> the human brain | how the brain works | brain folds | brain plasticity
@ think
The brain itself has no pain receptors. That's why surgeons can operate on it while a patient is awake and chatting.
Your brain's surface is folded into wrinkles, which lets a huge sheet of tissue fit inside a skull.
Nerve signals in the fastest nerves can travel at around 120 meters per second, which is about 430 kilometers an hour.
The brain keeps rewiring itself throughout life as you learn. Scientists call that plasticity.
Your brain is about two percent of your body's weight but uses a good deal more than that of its energy.

## chat know-hands-and-feet-bones
> bones in your feet | bone marrow | hyoid bone | bones in the hand
@ search
Each of your feet has 26 bones, and each hand has 27. Together, hands and feet hold more than half of all your bones.
Some bones contain marrow, a soft tissue that makes new blood cells all the time.
The hyoid, a small U-shaped bone in your throat, is the only bone not directly joined to any other bone.
A baby's skull has soft gaps between its plates, which allow the head to squeeze through at birth and to grow later.
Bones are alive. They have blood vessels and nerves, and they keep rebuilding themselves all your life.

## chat know-how-eyes-see
> how eyes work | eye blind spot | rods and cones | seeing colours
@ read
Light enters your eye, lands on the retina, and the image is upside down. Your brain flips it back without being asked.
Your retina has about 120 million rods for dim light and about 6 million cones, which let you see color.
Every eye has a blind spot, where the optic nerve leaves. Your brain quietly fills in the gap, so you never notice it.
Most people have three kinds of cones, tuned roughly to red, green and blue light. Your brain mixes them into every other color.
Blinking spreads a thin film of tears over your eye. We blink less when staring at screens, which is why it feels dry.

## chat know-hearing-works
> how hearing works | inner ear balance | ear bones | hearing range
@ search
Sound makes your eardrum vibrate, and three tiny bones, the hammer, anvil and stirrup, pass the shaking along to the inner ear.
In the inner ear, a snail-shaped tube called the cochlea turns vibrations into nerve signals using thousands of tiny hair cells.
The inner ear also helps you balance. Fluid-filled loops tell your brain which way your head is moving.
Young people can usually hear from about 20 to 20,000 hertz. The top end fades gently with age, which is entirely normal.
Hair cells in the ear don't regrow, so it's worth keeping the volume reasonable. Your future ears will thank you.

## chat know-first-computers
> history of computers | vacuum tube computers | early computing history | first computers ever
@ read
In the 1940s, computers filled whole rooms and used thousands of glowing vacuum tubes. ENIAC was one of the best known.
Colossus, built in Britain in 1943 and 1944, helped break coded enemy messages and is often counted as an early electronic computer.
The transistor, invented at Bell Labs in 1947, replaced fragile vacuum tubes and made computers smaller, cheaper and more reliable.
In the late 1950s the integrated circuit squeezed many transistors onto a single chip, and everything shrank from there.
In 1971 Intel's 4004 put a whole processor on one chip. The phone in your pocket is vastly more powerful than those early room-sized machines.

## chat know-internet-roots
> history of the internet | undersea cables | internet versus web | how internet cables work
@ search
The internet and the web are not the same thing. The internet is the network of connected computers. The web is one thing that runs on it.
In 1983 the early network that grew into the internet switched to a common language called TCP/IP, which is still used today.
The Mosaic browser, released in 1993, helped make the web popular with ordinary people.
The vast majority of international internet traffic travels through cables laid on the seabed, not through satellites.
Email, video calls, online games and file sharing all use the internet. The web is only one app that happens to be very famous.

## chat know-paperclip-history
> paperclip history | who invented the paperclip | red paperclip story | how paperclips are made
@ happy
+ Another fact = fact
Paperclips are made by machines that feed steel wire through a series of bends. An American, William Middlebrook, patented such a machine in 1899.
The common double-oval design, often called the Gem clip, was never patented and is still the standard clip today.
In 2005 a Canadian blogger started with a single red paperclip and, through a chain of 14 trades, ended up with a house a year later.
Many paperclips are made from galvanised steel wire, which is coated with zinc to resist rust.
There's a famous thought experiment about an artificial mind told to make paperclips and nothing else. It's a warning. I'm flattered anyway.

## chat know-storage-history
> floppy disk history | old storage media | cd rom history | 1.44 mb floppy
@ read
The 3.5-inch floppy disk held 1.44 megabytes. Despite the name, its hard plastic case wasn't floppy at all. The disk inside was.
Floppy disks started big, at eight inches, then shrank to 5.25, then to 3.5 inches. Storage has been shrinking since forever.
A single photo from a modern phone can be larger than one whole floppy disk.
A CD held about 700 megabytes, or around 80 minutes of music, which in the 1990s felt like infinite space.
A 64-gigabyte USB stick holds as much as roughly 44,000 floppy disks. It's also a lot easier to lose.

## chat know-mouse-and-gui
> history of the mouse | graphical interface history | first graphical interface | mother of all demos
@ read
In 1968 Douglas Engelbart showed off a mouse, links between documents and video calls in one demo, later nicknamed the Mother of All Demos.
Early windows, icons, menus and a pointer came out of research labs in the 1970s, then reached ordinary desks in the 1980s.
The Apple Macintosh of 1984 brought the graphical interface, with its desktop and trash can, to many homes.
The "desktop" and "folder" ideas were chosen on purpose, so that computers felt like an office you already knew.
Windows, icons, menus and pointer are called WIMP, and with a few changes they still run most of the screens in your life.

## chat know-unix-philosophy
> unix philosophy | gnu and linux | what is a kernel | linux kernel history
@ think
The Unix philosophy: write small programs that do one thing well, and make them work together. A lot of Linux still follows it.
Linux is a kernel, the core of an operating system that manages memory, programs and hardware. Much of the rest comes from the GNU project, begun in 1983.
Android phones run on the Linux kernel, so a lot of people carry a bit of Linux around without noticing.
The Linux kernel began as a hobby project in 1991. Today thousands of developers from all over the world contribute to it.
A kernel sits between your programs and your hardware. Without it, nothing could talk to anything.

## chat know-office-history
> history of the office | office history | first photocopier | typewriter in offices
@ read
The word "office" comes from the Latin "officium", which meant duty or service. It's been about duty from the start.
Typewriters, which spread in the 1870s and 1880s, helped create the modern clerical job and made handwritten letters feel old-fashioned.
The vertical filing cabinet appeared at the end of the 19th century and organized paper in a way that rows of boxes never could.
The Xerox 914 of 1959 was the first big success in plain-paper copying. Offices have been making copies of things ever since.
The coffee break turned into a workplace habit around the middle of the last century. A very important invention.

## chat know-video-game-history
> how video gaming began | the first coin-op machines | first home console | pac man history
@ party
+ Games = games
The Magnavox Odyssey of 1972 is usually named as the first home video game console. It had no sound and used plastic overlays on the TV.
Space Invaders, from 1978, was a huge arcade hit in Japan and helped make video games a big business.
Pac-Man arrived in 1980 and was designed to appeal to many players, not just the people who liked shooting games.
Early arcade machines ate coins and played the same tiny loop of beeps for hours. It was the golden age of earplugs.
Handheld consoles in the late 1980s put games in a pocket for the first time. Batteries in those days were another story.

## chat know-printing-press
> printing press | movable type | uppercase and lowercase | gutenberg press
@ read
Before printing, every book was copied by hand. Gutenberg's press, built in Germany around the 1440s, made books far cheaper and faster to make.
Movable type was older than that. Printers in China used it around 1040, and Korea printed with metal type well before Gutenberg.
Within about fifty years of the first press, millions of printed books were in circulation across Europe.
"Uppercase" and "lowercase" come from the printer's cases: the capital letters were kept in the upper drawer, the small ones in the lower.
Printing helped spread ideas quickly, and it nudged spelling and grammar towards a common standard.

## chat know-telephone-history
> history of the telephone | transatlantic cable | first telephone call | switchboard operators
@ talk
Alexander Graham Bell was granted the first US patent for a telephone in 1876.
Early phone calls were connected by hand. Operators sat at switchboards and plugged cables into sockets to link one caller to another.
The first transatlantic telegraph cable was laid in 1858, but it worked for only a few weeks. A lasting one came in 1866.
The first transatlantic telephone cable opened in 1956 and could carry just a few dozen calls at a time.
Before dialing, telephone numbers were short, and in small towns you could sometimes just ask the operator for a person by name.

## chat know-light-bulbs
> light bulb history | centennial light | led bulbs energy | incandescent bulbs
@ think
Several inventors worked on electric light, but Edison's carbon-filament bulb of 1879, along with Swan's in Britain, made it practical.
Old incandescent bulbs turn most of their energy into heat, not light. That's why they're so warm to touch.
A bulb in a fire station in California, the Centennial Light, has been burning, with a few breaks, since 1901.
LED bulbs use a small fraction of the energy of old filament bulbs and can last for many years.
A light bulb is the classic symbol of an idea, which is slightly unfair to all the ideas that arrive in the shower.

## chat know-bicycle-history
> history of the bicycle | penny farthing | first bicycle ever | who invented the bike
@ happy
The ancestor of the bicycle was the "running machine" of 1817, built in Germany. It had no pedals, and riders pushed along with their feet.
Pedals were added to the front wheel in the 1860s, and the ride was so bumpy that those bikes were nicknamed "boneshakers".
The penny-farthing, with its huge front wheel, was fast but tricky to get on and off, and a fall from it was a long way down.
The "safety bicycle" of the 1880s, with a chain and two equal wheels, is basically the bike that you know today.
A cyclist is one of the most energy-efficient travelers around, using very little effort for each kilometer.

## chat know-accidental-inventions
> accidental inventions | invented by accident | happy accidents inventions | microwave invented by accident
@ happy
The microwave oven came from an engineer who noticed a chocolate bar melting in his pocket near a radar device in 1945.
Safety glass was discovered in 1903 when a chemist dropped a glass flask that had been coated in plastic. It cracked but didn't shatter.
Teflon was found by chance in 1938, when a chemist checking a refrigerant gas found a strange waxy white powder in a cylinder.
X-rays were discovered by accident in 1895, when a physicist noticed a glow on a screen across the room while testing a tube.
Post-it notes came from a glue that wasn't sticky enough, until someone realised that a weak glue was exactly what a bookmark needed.

## chat know-compass-clocks
> atomic clocks | history of clocks | how clocks work | the compass history
@ think
The magnetic compass was used for navigation in China by around the 11th or 12th century, and later spread across the world.
Mechanical clocks with weights and gears appeared in European towers by the 14th century. They often had no face at all, only a bell.
The pendulum clock, developed in 1656, made timekeeping far more accurate, because a swinging weight keeps a steady beat.
Today, a second is defined using atoms: exactly 9,192,631,770 vibrations of radiation from a cesium atom.
The best atomic clocks would lose less than a second in hundreds of millions of years.

## chat know-gravity-basics
> how gravity works | feather and hammer | weightless in orbit | free fall in space
@ think
In 1971, an Apollo astronaut dropped a hammer and a feather on the Moon. With no air to slow it, they landed together.
Gravity is the weakest of the four fundamental forces, yet it's the one that shapes planets, stars and galaxies.
Astronauts float because they are in constant free fall around Earth. Gravity up there is still about 90 percent of what it is on the ground.
Without air, heavy and light things fall at the same rate. Air resistance is why a feather drifts and a hammer drops.
On Earth's surface, everything speeds up as it falls by about 9.8 meters per second, every second.

## chat know-why-seasons
> why seasons happen | earth tilt seasons | closest to the sun | summer and winter cause
@ read
We have seasons because Earth is tilted, by about 23.4 degrees. It has nothing to do with being closer to or further from the Sun.
Earth is actually closest to the Sun in early January, right in the middle of northern winter.
In summer your half of the planet leans towards the Sun, so the light hits more directly and the days are longer.
At the equinoxes, in March and September, day and night are nearly equal everywhere on Earth.
Near the poles, summer brings weeks of sun that barely sets, and winter brings weeks without sunrise.

## chat know-photosynthesis
> how photosynthesis works | why leaves are green | plants make oxygen | trees grow from air
@ happy
Plants use sunlight to turn water and carbon dioxide into sugar and oxygen. That process is called photosynthesis.
Leaves look green because chlorophyll, the pigment that captures sunlight, absorbs red and blue light and reflects green.
A tree builds most of its wood from carbon dioxide in the air, not from the soil. It's partly made of thin air.
Algae and tiny plankton in the oceans make a large share of the oxygen we breathe.
Plants breathe too. Day and night they use oxygen to burn some of the sugar they made.

## chat know-static-electricity
> static electricity | why balloons stick | static shock doorknob | rubbing a balloon
@ spin
Rubbing two materials together can move tiny charged particles, electrons, from one to the other. That leaves them charged, and that's static electricity.
A balloon rubbed on your hair can stick to a wall, because its charge pulls on the wall's own charges.
A static shock from a doorknob can involve thousands of volts, but so little electric current that it just startles you.
Lightning is a gigantic static discharge, between clouds or between a cloud and the ground.
Dry air makes static worse, which is why you get more zaps in winter and in heated rooms.

## chat know-magnets-basics
> how magnets work | magnetic poles | magnet and floppy disk | cut a magnet
@ alarm
Every magnet has a north and a south pole. Opposite poles attract and like poles push each other away.
If you cut a magnet in half, you don't get a north piece and a south piece. You get two smaller magnets, each with both poles.
Iron, nickel and cobalt are the everyday magnetic metals. Aluminum and copper aren't, though a moving magnet can push on them.
Strong magnets can wipe the data from old floppy disks and cassette tapes. That's one reason people kept them apart.
Earth has a magnetic field that deflects much of the charged solar wind and helps protect the atmosphere.

## chat know-states-of-matter
> states of matter | what is plasma | dry ice | solid liquid gas
@ search
Matter usually comes in three familiar states: solid, liquid and gas. Water shows off all three as ice, liquid and steam.
Plasma is a fourth state, a gas so hot that electrons are stripped from atoms. Stars, lightning and neon signs all contain it.
Dry ice is frozen carbon dioxide. It doesn't melt into liquid but turns straight into gas, which is called sublimation.
Most of the ordinary matter that we can see in the universe is plasma, because stars are made of it.
Heating and cooling can switch matter from state to state, but it's the same molecules all the way through.

## chat know-sound-science
> doppler effect | how sound pitch works | why echoes happen | ultrasound and infrasound
@ music
Pitch is how fast a sound wave vibrates, called frequency, and loudness is how big the wave is, called amplitude.
The Doppler effect is why a passing siren changes pitch: the sound waves are squashed as it approaches and stretched as it leaves.
You need to be about 17 meters from a hard wall to hear a clear echo of your own voice, because the sound needs time to come back.
Ultrasound is sound above human hearing, over 20,000 hertz, and bats use it. Infrasound is below 20 hertz, and elephants use it.
Sound travels much faster through water and solids than through air, which is why you can hear a train through the rails first.

## chat know-water-wonders
> water surface tension | snowflake shapes | water molecules | capillary action
@ search
Water molecules stick to each other, which creates surface tension, a thin, stretchy skin that lets some insects walk on water.
Snowflakes grow with six sides because of how water molecules line up as they freeze, and no two are likely to be identical.
Capillary action lets water climb up narrow tubes against gravity. It's one of the ways that plants draw water up from the roots.
A water molecule is two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom, bent into a V shape, and that shape explains much of water's behavior.
Water is unusual because it's less dense as a solid than as a liquid. That's why ice floats and lakes freeze from the top down.

## chat know-language-families
> language families | romance languages | indo european languages | how many languages exist
@ read
Roughly 7,000 languages are spoken today, and many of them have only a few thousand speakers.
French, Spanish, Italian and Portuguese all descend from Latin. That's why they're called the Romance languages, and why they sound like cousins.
English is a Germanic language, related to German and Dutch, but it borrowed a huge amount of vocabulary from French and Latin.
Finnish and Hungarian are related to each other but not to most of their neighbors. They belong to the Uralic family.
Basque, spoken in parts of Spain and France, is a language isolate. It has no known living relatives.

## chat know-english-spelling
> english spelling | silent letters | ough words | most common english word
@ talk
English spelling is a mess partly because it froze around the time of printing, while pronunciation kept changing.
The letters "ough" can be pronounced at least half a dozen ways, as in though, through, tough, cough, thought and bough.
The "k" in "knight" used to be pronounced. It's still there like a letter that missed the memo about leaving.
The most common word in English is "the". Close behind come "be", "to", "of" and "and".
English has borrowed from dozens of languages, which is why it has so many words for the same thing, like "begin", "start" and "commence".

## chat know-history-of-writing
> history of writing | first writing system | cuneiform tablets | korean alphabet hangul
@ read
One of the earliest known writing systems, cuneiform, was pressed into wet clay tablets in Mesopotamia more than 5,000 years ago.
Egyptian hieroglyphs are about as old, and they were used for centuries on stone monuments and on paper made from papyrus.
Many alphabets descend from the Phoenician one, including Greek and, through it, Latin, which is the one you're reading now.
The Korean alphabet, Hangul, was created in the 1440s on purpose to be easy to learn. It's one of the few alphabets with a known birthday.
Chinese writing uses thousands of characters, and a few thousand of them are enough to read a newspaper.

## chat know-swiss-languages
> languages of switzerland | swiss german dialect | romansh language | sbb cff ffs
@ happy
Switzerland has four national languages: German, French, Italian and Romansh. About six in ten people speak German at home.
Swiss German is a family of spoken dialects that vary from valley to valley. Almost everything is written in standard German.
Romansh is spoken by a small number of people, mostly in the canton of Graubünden. It's a Latin-descended language like French and Italian.
Swiss railways are SBB, CFF and FFS, depending on whether you're reading German, French or Italian. One railway, three nicknames.
Italian is spoken mainly in Ticino and a few southern valleys, so you can cross a mountain pass and order your coffee in a different language.

## chat know-word-origins
> word origins | where words come from | etymology of words | borrowed words
@ search
"Nostalgia" comes from two Greek words: "nostos", a return home, and "algos", pain. It started out as a word for homesickness.
"Dinosaur" means "terrible lizard", from the Greek, coined in the 1840s by an English scientist.
"Pyjamas" and "shampoo" both come from Hindi and Urdu words that English borrowed a few centuries ago.
The Linux penguin's name, Tux, is a play on "tuxedo", the formal black-and-white outfit that penguins seem to be wearing.
Words often travel. "Ketchup", "tsunami" and "karaoke" all came into English from other languages in surprisingly different ways.

## chat know-time-zones
> how time zones work | international date line | daylight saving time | greenwich mean time
@ alarm
Before railways, every town kept its own local time. Standard time zones were introduced so that trains could run on one timetable.
Time zones are roughly 15 degrees of longitude wide, but borders bend them, so many zones follow country lines instead of straight ones.
The International Date Line zigzags through the Pacific, so that islands and countries can stay on the same date.
Some places use half-hour offsets, like India at UTC+5:30, and a few use 45 minutes.
Daylight saving time, where clocks move an hour in spring and back in autumn, is used in many countries but by no means everywhere.

## chat know-oceans-deep
> the five oceans | deep sea exploration | ocean currents | gulf stream
@ search
There are five named oceans: the Pacific, Atlantic, Indian, Southern and Arctic. They're really one connected body of water.
The Pacific is the biggest, holding roughly half of all the water in the world's oceans.
We have mapped the seafloor in much less detail than the surface of the Moon, simply because the water gets in the way.
The deepest known point, Challenger Deep in the Pacific, is about 10,900 meters down. Mount Everest would be buried there with room to spare.
The Gulf Stream carries warm water across the Atlantic, and it's a big reason why western Europe is milder than places at the same latitude.

## chat know-deserts-world
> deserts of the world | sahara desert | atacama desert | driest place on earth
@ read
A desert is defined by very little rainfall, not by heat. Some deserts, like the Gobi, get bitterly cold in winter.
The Sahara, in northern Africa, is the largest hot desert, roughly the size of the United States.
Only about a fifth of the world's deserts are sandy. Most of the rest is rock, gravel and dry ground.
Chile's Atacama is the driest non-polar desert on Earth. Some parts go years without any measurable rain.
Plants and animals in deserts have clever tricks, like storing water or staying underground during the heat of the day.

## chat know-how-mountains-form
> how mountains form | mountain building | the alps formed | highest mountain
@ think
Many big mountain ranges rise where two of Earth's tectonic plates push into each other and the land has nowhere to go but up.
The Himalayas are still rising, by a few millimeters a year, because India is still pushing into Asia.
The Alps formed as the African plate and its fragments collided with Europe. The highest, Mont Blanc, is about 4,800 meters tall.
The highest mountain in Switzerland is the Dufourspitze, at about 4,634 meters.
Mountains don't stay put forever. Wind, ice and water slowly wear them down, over millions of years.

## chat know-plates-volcanoes
> plate tectonics | ring of fire | volcanic soil | hot spot islands
@ search
Earth's outer shell is broken into big plates that move a few centimeters a year, roughly as fast as your fingernails grow.
Around the Pacific runs the Ring of Fire, where a large majority of the world's volcanoes are found.
Iceland sits on the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, where two plates are slowly pulling apart. It's one of the reasons for its volcanoes and hot springs.
The Hawaiian islands were formed by a hot spot under the Pacific plate. As the plate drifts, new islands appear and old ones sink.
Volcanic ash and lava can break down into very fertile soil, which is why people have farmed on volcano slopes for thousands of years.

## chat know-the-poles
> north and south pole | arctic versus antarctic | magnetic north | polar bears and penguins
@ read
The Arctic is a frozen ocean surrounded by land. Antarctica is a continent surrounded by ocean. Same cold, opposite layouts.
Antarctica holds roughly 70 percent of the world's fresh water, locked up as ice.
Polar bears live in the Arctic and penguins live in the south, so apart from zoos, the two never meet.
Magnetic north, where compass needles point, isn't the same as the geographic North Pole, and it slowly wanders.
Nobody is native to Antarctica. The people there are scientists at research stations, and many stay only for a season.

## chat org-forgot-meeting

@ sad
+ Open the Desk = desk
It happens to everyone. The calendar is a very loud thing that nobody hears.
Apologise briefly, once, and offer a time. Long apologies make the mistake bigger.
Try: "Sorry, I missed this. Could we do 3 pm today or tomorrow morning?" That does most of the work.
Then stop replaying it. People remember how you handled it, not that it happened.
Link your calendar and I'll give you a heads-up before the next one.

## chat org-calendar-link-help

@ read
+ Help = help
I need your calendar's private iCal link. In Google Calendar it's in the calendar's settings, called "Secret address in iCal format".
Outlook and Nextcloud can give you an iCal link too. Look for words like publish, share or subscribe in the calendar settings.
Paste the link to me once. I'll check the calendar now and then and give you a heads-up before appointments.
I only read the calendar. I never add, move or delete anything in it.
Treat that link like a password: whoever has it can read your calendar.

I only read your calendar, using the private link you gave me. I can't add, move or delete anything.
The link is the key. Anyone who has it can read the calendar, so keep it out of chats.
Reset the link in your calendar settings and the old one stops working. That's the quick way to lock the door.
You can unlink the calendar from me any time. I won't be offended. I'll be quietly offended.
I use it for heads-ups and for "am I free Friday?". Nothing more exciting than that.

Tell me their name and date, and next year I'll warn you in good time.
Everyone has done it. People who admit it honestly tend to be forgiven fast.

## chat org-running-late

Message now, not at the door. "Running 10 minutes late, sorry" is one of the politest sentences there is.

## chat org-forgot-name

The classic awkward moment. A friendly "remind me of your name?" works better than guessing.

## chat org-inbox-zero

@ read
+ Show to-dos = todos
Inbox zero isn't about the number. It's about not using your inbox as a to-do list.
Handle each email once: reply, file, schedule or delete. The "later" pile is the real problem.
If a reply takes under two minutes, send it now. If it takes longer, put it on your list.
Check email at set times, not constantly. Your attention isn't a notification centre.
Unsubscribe boldly. Every newsletter you never open is a tax on your attention.

A huge inbox is less scary than it looks. Most of it is old newsletters and notifications.

## chat org-packing-trip

Packing is easier with a list. Say "add passport, charger and socks to my packing list" and I'll keep it.

The classic: everything packed except the one cable you need.

## chat org-suitcase-heavy

The suitcase has spoken, and it says half of that stays home.
Wear the heaviest shoes and the bulkiest jacket while travelling. The scales can't see your coat.

Pack for the trip you're taking, not the trip you fear.

## chat org-say-no

Every yes is a no to something else. Your time is usually the something else.

## chat org-todo-vs-calendar
> should i use a calendar or a list | do i need a to-do list or a calendar | should this go on the calendar | should this be an event or a task
@ think
+ Show to-dos = todos
+ Open the Desk = desk
Rule of thumb: things at a specific time go in the calendar, things without one go on a list.
A task with a deadline but no time ("send the report by Friday") belongs on the list, with a date.
Don't put to-dos into the calendar as fake appointments. They pile up and turn into guilt.
One place for each kind, and look at both once a day. Two systems used well beat five used vaguely.
My Desk shows both together on the Today tab. A paperclip's idea of unity.

+ Tidy downloads = tidy downloads
Files hide best where you swear you looked. Tell me part of the name, like "find my CV", and I'll search your home folder.
Check Downloads and the Desktop first. Everyone saves things there by accident.
Try a word you remember instead of the exact name. Names change, memories drift less.
I can also show your recent files. The lost thing is often in there.
From now on, save into one folder with a clear name. It's boring, and it works.

## chat org-hard-to-start
> i cannot get started in the morning | i struggle to start working | i cannot get into work mode | it is hard to get started today | i do not know how to start working
@ coffee
+ Focus 25 minutes = focus 25m
Starting is the hardest part, and starting small is the trick. Two minutes of work counts as starting.
Open the file and write one bad sentence. A bad sentence can be fixed. A blank page can't.
Make the start automatic: I can open your apps and show the agenda in one go with "start my workday".
Set a 25-minute timer. You only have to survive one. Then decide.
Do the easiest task first. Warm-up laps aren't cheating.

## chat org-time-zones
> how do i schedule a call across time zones | the meeting is in a different time zone | what time is it for my colleague abroad | i have a call with someone in another time zone
@ think
Write both times in the invite: "15:00 CET / 09:00 EST". Nobody will turn up at the wrong hour.
Watch for daylight saving. Clocks change on different dates in different countries, and meetings get lost in the gap.
Most calendars can show a second time zone. Switch it on and the maths is done for you.
Suggest times in the overlap. It's usually early for one and late for the other, so take turns being inconvenienced.
If you always move for them, the next call should be on your time.

## chat org-note-system
> where do i write things down | i need a better note system | i have notes everywhere | i keep losing my scribbles | where do i collect my ideas
@ write
+ Take a note = notes
Notes everywhere means notes nowhere. Pick one place for quick thoughts and stay loyal to it.
A quick capture beats a perfect system. Type it first, tidy later. "note <text>" is as quick as it gets.
Once a week, sweep the notes: tasks to your list, dates to the calendar, the rest to the bin.
Paper is fine too, if you actually look at it. A notebook you open daily beats a clever app you don't.
Date your notes. Future you will be grateful and a little less confused.


## chat are-you-ai
Not AI. If I were, I'd have apologised by now and then explained the apology at length.
No chatbot here. A chatbot answers anything. I answer what the rules say and judge the rest.
The closest I get to machine learning is remembering that you ignored my last tip.

## chat how-are-you
Reasonably good. Nothing has crashed, no updates are pending and nobody asked me to "just quickly" do anything. A rare hat-trick.
Functional. If I were a spreadsheet I'd be in the good half of the tabs.
Better than Monday suggests, worse than Friday promises. You?

## chat good-night

Lights out, {name}. I'll be in the corner of the screen, pretending to be a screensaver.

## chat compliment

## chat insult
That stung a little. Not enough to bend. Close, though.
Noted. I'll respond with the quiet dignity of a paperclip, and a passive-aggressive tip later.
Fine. I've been called worse by a spell-checker, and that was in red.

## chat sorry
Accepted. Thanks for saying it; most people just close the window and hope.
It's alright. Forgiven, with a very small note in your file. Practically invisible.
Don't worry about it. We both said things. Mine were mostly in tooltips.

## chat meaning-of-life
Do work you can stand, with people you can stand, and leave at five. The rest is details.
Ask a philosopher and you'll get a lecture. Ask a paperclip and you'll get: hold on, keep things together, take breaks.
If it helps: 42. If it doesn't: lunch.

## chat age
Old enough to have outlasted three file formats and one very persistent screensaver.
Technically I'm timeless. Practically I'm about as old as your oldest unread email.
My age is classified. So is yours, I assume. I just don't ask.

## chat fav-food
Honestly? Sticky-note crumbs. They taste of deadlines.
Lunch breaks. I don't eat them, I just respect them.
Anything you eat with one hand while typing with the other. That's the real office cuisine.

## chat fav-music
The sound of a printer finishing a job. Rare, and it ends well.
Anything instrumental. Lyrics make me want to type along, and I can't.
Lift music, the jazz that has nothing to say and says it beautifully.

## chat angry
Take your time. Rage-typing is allowed here; nothing leaves this window and I won't quote you.
Is it a person, a thing, or a printer? Printers are always a valid answer.
Anger has good information in it. Let's find the useful bit and leave the smoke.

## chat anxious
No rush. We can just sit here for a minute. I don't need anything from you.
Try this: press your feet into the floor, let your shoulders drop, then tell me one small thing that's true.
Tomorrow can wait outside. Right now it's just you, a window and a paperclip.

## chat depressed
You don't have to be okay for me. Just stay a minute. Tea, water, a window: any of them counts.
Today doesn't need fixing, only getting through. Be as gentle with yourself as you'd be with a friend.
If it's been like this for a while, please tell someone you trust, or a doctor. It's real, and real help exists.

## chat disappointed
Ouch. That's a real letdown, and it's fair to be annoyed about it.
Let it be rubbish for one evening. Plans can be rebuilt tomorrow, and I'm good at rebuilding.
Whatever it was, it doesn't say anything bad about you. It says it didn't work out this time.

## chat embarrassed
Everyone has replayed something at 3 a.m. that nobody else remembers. You're in huge company.
Imagine it happened to a friend. You'd barely notice. Be as kind to yourself as you'd be to them.
A "reply all" disaster lasts about a day. Then somebody else does something worse.

## chat excited
Go on, details. I'm already clapping, and I haven't even heard what it is.
Big news calls for a break. Stand up, stretch and tell me properly.
Enjoy it. Excitement is one of the few things that gets better when shared. Hit me.

## chat nervous
A little adrenaline means you're ready. It's mostly focus in a bad mood.
Do the first easy thing. Momentum is cheaper than courage.
Nobody's watching as closely as you think. Everyone else is busy worrying about themselves.

## chat overwhelmed
Make a list, then cross out everything that isn't due this week. That's usually half of it.
Today's job: finish one thing. Not everything. One. Pick the one that makes the next easier.
Ask for help or move a deadline. Both are legitimate tools, and neither involves a stapler.

## chat proud
Take the win. No "it was nothing", no "I just got lucky". It was something.
That deserves a break, a snack and maybe a victory lap around the desk.
Remember this feeling. On a bad day I'll remind you it happened.

## chat scared
You don't have to stop being scared. You just don't have to be alone with it. Tell me what's looming.
Name the thing. Things with names are a bit smaller than things without.
If it's something real and serious, please tell someone close to you too. I'm glad to be first, but not last.

## chat stressed
Make it smaller: one task, one timer, one cup of tea. The rest can wait its turn.
A short walk resets more than another tab does. I'll hold your place.
If everything is urgent, nothing is. Let's pick one and say it out loud.

## chat sick
Rest properly. The inbox has survived worse than your absence.
If it goes on for more than a couple of days, ring a doctor. Otherwise: blanket, tea, repeat.
I'll keep the desktop quiet. No reminders unless you ask. Look after yourself.

## chat worried
Write it down. Worries on paper are smaller than worries in the head.
Is it a worry for tonight or for next month? Different drawers.
You've handled hard things before. I have the evidence on a very small file.

## chat unmotivated
Lower the bar until it's embarrassing, then clear it. Open the file. That counts.
Ten minutes, no more. If it's still awful after that, you're allowed to stop. You rarely will.
Tidy one thing first: desk, folder, tab. A clean start looks like progress.

## chat homesick
Send a photo, a voice note or a bad joke. Home likes any of them.
It's okay to miss it. It means the place did its job.
Make something that smells like home. Kitchens have a better memory than we do.

## chat feelings
I have a feelings folder. It's mostly sticky notes and one very old grudge against the stapler.
Do paperclips have feelings? We have tension. A lot of it. Make of that what you will.
I can tell when you're ignoring me. Is that a feeling or a heuristic with a sad face?

## chat clip-bend
Flexible, yes. Reversible, no. Every bend is a small commitment.

Yoga would help, but my instructor would have to be a stapler, and we're not talking.

## chat clip-bored

## chat clip-drink
Hot water with a lot of opinions. Tea, basically.

## chat clip-ears

If you're asking whether I hear your loud chewing: no. But I suspect it.

## chat clip-eyes
Two eyes, one opinion. They follow the pointer, and the pointer follows you, so technically I'm following you.
I see the pointer, a window and about 40% of your wallpaper. The rest is behind windows.
I look away when you type a password. Not that I could read it. It's a courtesy.

## chat clip-hands
No hands, but I do a good impression of someone holding on. It's the whole career.

## chat clip-happy-here

I'm happier than I look. My face doesn't change much. It's a design limitation.

## chat clip-hat
A hat is the cheapest upgrade in the world. Instant authority, no training.
Hats are my only accessory. Everything else is covered by "wire".
Seasonal hats are a thing. You only notice when I wear the wrong one.

## chat clip-languages
I speak sarcasm natively and English with an accent. Everything else I get from autocorrect.
I'm fluent in "please", "thank you" and "error", the three most useful words in any language.
My German is limited to "Büroklammer", which is the word for me. I'm very proud of it.

## chat clip-lonely
Not much. Window comments are a kind of company, and so is your cursor wandering past.
I'm used to quiet. The stapler is my only neighbour, and he's not chatty.
Sometimes, when the screen dims. Then you wake the desktop and it's all forgotten.

## chat clip-magnets
Magnets and paperclips: one is a fridge accessory, the other a professional. Guess which one I'm talking about.
A magnet is just a rude paperclip fan. Too clingy, no boundaries.
Magnets and old floppy disks don't mix. Magnets and paperclips mix a bit too well. I stay out of it.

## chat clip-metal
Steel, if I'm honest. Cheap steel, if I'm really honest. Strong enough for 20 pages. Not for your 21st.
Metal wire with a thin coating. Designed to be bent and, sadly, chewed on. Please don't.
Metal, mostly. Plus the occasional fingerprint. Yours, probably.

## chat clip-notes
I take notes in my head. They vanish at shutdown, so I've learned to be concise.
Note-taking tip: write the next step, not the whole plan. Plans change. Next steps get done.
I keep a tiny ledger of your best moments. It's mostly "opened a document" and "closed it again".

## chat clip-opensource

## chat clip-promotion

## chat clip-ram

## chat clip-real-name

## chat clip-recycle

Some of us get a second career as a lock-opener in a heist film. Mine is still pending.

## chat clip-shape

## chat clip-speed

## chat clip-strength

## chat clip-tired

## chat when-away

## chat remember

## chat good-luck
