## chat congrats-to-me
Well done, you. Take the compliment, the biscuit and ten minutes off.
Congratulations. I'm so proud I'm almost on time with a joke.
A small round of applause, delivered with great sincerity and no hands.

## chat work-bored-at-work
Boredom at work usually means the real task is hiding. Ask what's waiting behind the one you're avoiding.
Go for a lap of the office. Walking looks like thinking, and it actually helps.
Learn one keyboard shortcut. A small, permanent upgrade to a slow Tuesday.

## chat work-boss-feedback
Feedback delivered at 4:55 on a Friday is a special kind of cruelty. Sleep on it before replying.
Sandwich feedback exists: nice, hard, nice. Look for the filling. It's usually the useful bit.
If the feedback is vague, ask for one example. Vague criticism is just worry in a suit.

## chat work-boss-micromanager
Ask for the outcome and the deadline, then offer a weekly check-in instead of an hourly one. People prefer a plan to a shadow.
If they ask for updates constantly, post one proactively. A shared doc beats forty pings.
Micromanagement is often fear in a manager costume. Calm the fear, lose the costume.

## chat work-boss-new
Find out how they like to be told bad news. Everybody has a preference, and it's rarely "at the last minute".
A new boss is a fresh start for them too. They're also quietly googling what everybody does.
Don't judge the first week. Most people are just trying not to be fired.

## chat work-burnout
Rest counts as work. So does saying no to one thing this week. Start there.
Take your leave, even if it isn't convenient. It never will be. Take it anyway.
If it's been going on a while, a doctor or counsellor is a smart next step, not a weak one.

## chat work-camera-off
Rule of thumb: camera on for introductions and key moments, off for the long stretches where you're just listening.
Camera fatigue is real, mostly because you're watching a tiny version of yourself all day.
If someone insists on cameras, ask "what's it for?" Often nobody remembers.

## chat work-coffee-machine-broken
A broken coffee machine drops productivity by about 40 percent. I made that number up, but you felt it.
Meanwhile, tea. A kettle and a bag. It's the backup of civilisation.
Put a sign on it with a name and a date, not just "out of order". Someone will be motivated by shame.

## chat work-commute-traffic
Use the jam for something small and useful: a podcast, a call to a relative, a playlist that's actually decent.
A commute is just a very slow meeting with traffic as the agenda.
If you can, leave ten minutes earlier or later. The jam moves; so can you.

## chat work-commute-train-late
Public transport is an honest teacher: it shows you what you can't control and what's in your bag.
Send the "running late" message the moment you know. It costs ten seconds and saves a lot of eyebrows.
Delay compensation exists in many places. Check the app. A refund is a refund.

## chat work-coworker-credit
Credit theft rarely happens to loud people. Put your name on things early, in writing.
Send a short summary email after big pieces of work: "Here's what I did." It's polite and permanent.
Don't confront them in front of others. A private, calm chat gets better results and fewer sulks.

## chat work-coworker-loud
Headphones and a "do not disturb" status do wonders. A paperclip on your monitor does nothing, but it looks supportive.
Try asking for quiet hours in a shared space. Most people agree when it's an hour, not a personality.
Some people talk loudly because they're lonely, others because they're on a call. Knowing which helps.

## chat work-deadline-missed
First, tell the person waiting. Silence makes a late thing feel later. Then give a new date you can actually hit.
Ask which part matters most and send that first. Partial and on time beats perfect and late.
Missing one deadline rarely ends a career. Missing them quietly does.

## chat work-deadline-moved
When a deadline moves up, ask what moves down. Priorities are a seesaw, not a stack.
Split it into a Friday version and an ideal version. Ship the first, polish the second.
Short notice isn't your emergency, but it can still be your problem. Write down what you were told, and when.

## chat work-delegation
Write down what "done" looks like in three bullet points. If you can't, you're not delegating, you're hoping.
Pick the right person, not the free one. Free people are free for a reason.
Let them make mistakes small enough to fix. That's how they learn and how you sleep.

## chat work-difficult-client
Write it in this order: what happened, what we can do, when. It disarms most angry emails.
Don't promise what you can't do. A calm "no" with an alternative beats a panicked "yes".
After the call, send a summary: "As discussed…". It turns a heated chat into a record.

## chat work-email-overload
Turn off new-mail notifications. The email will still be there when you look; you'll just be calmer.
Subject lines that say "FYI" can wait. Subject lines that say "URGENT" can wait too, mostly.
Reply with a single sentence. Long answers invite long replies.

## chat work-email-tone
Cut "just" and "sorry to bother you" where you don't mean them. Keep "please" and "thanks"; those pull their weight.
An exclamation mark is cheaper than a paragraph of warmth. Use one, not seven.
Read it as if you were the recipient on a Monday morning. Then send.

## chat work-expense-report
Do it weekly, not monthly. Five receipts are easy; fifty are a hostage situation.
Write the purpose next to each receipt while you remember it. "Lunch" and "client lunch" are different things.
Finance doesn't hate you. They hate missing receipts, so give them what they ask for.

## chat work-feeling-stuck
List three things you've learned this year. An empty list means you've found the problem. A long one means you're less stuck than you feel.
Talk to someone two steps ahead of you. Not for advice, just to see what's on the road.
Sometimes it's not the job that's stuck, it's the Tuesdays. Change one routine and see.

## chat work-freelance
Raise your rates a little every year. Clients rarely notice, and your bank account will.
Say no to work that's a poor fit. A "no" now saves a nasty email later.
Keep business and personal money separate from the start. Tax season will thank you.

## chat work-friday-afternoon
Do the pleasant small tasks now: renaming files, writing Monday's list. Save the big thinking for Monday's brain.
The Friday email you're about to send can almost always wait until Monday. Draft it, don't send it.
Clear your desk for the weekend. Monday-you will be unreasonably grateful.

## chat work-headphones
Over-ear headphones say "busy". Earbuds say "maybe". No headphones say "come and tell me about your weekend".
A playlist you know by heart is best for focus. New music wants your attention.
Don't leave noise-cancelling on during a fire drill. A joke, but also practical.

## chat work-imposter
The people who worry about being frauds are usually the ones checking their work twice.
Write down one thing you figured out this week that you didn't know last week. That's the job.
Say "I don't know yet" a few times at work. The sky doesn't fall, and people trust you more.

## chat work-interruptions
Put up a sign: "Heads down until 11." It works on people the way a closed door does.
Batch the small stuff: answer all quick questions at 11 and 3. The rest of the day belongs to you.
A focus timer is a polite way of saying "not now". I can set one: just ask.

## chat work-interview-failed
Ask for feedback if they offer it. Not all do, but a polite email costs nothing and sometimes brings gold.
A "no" often means "not this one, not now". The role may have been a poor fit for both sides.
Do one nice thing for yourself today. You did a hard thing, and it counts regardless of the result.

## chat work-interview-prep
Prepare a one-minute "tell me about yourself". It's the first question, and it goes better if it isn't improvised.
Look up your interviewers if you know their names, so you can ask something sensible. Keep it professional.
Eat something beforehand. A rumbling stomach is a surprisingly loud colleague.

## chat work-interview-salary-question
Don't say a number first if you can avoid it. "What's the range for this role?" is a perfectly good opening move.
Practise saying your number out loud. It's amazing how many people mumble.
Get the offer in writing before you celebrate. Celebrating is allowed afterwards, with cake.

## chat work-it-ticket
Give the ticket a clear title. "Laptop broken" gets a queue. "Cannot connect to VPN since 9:15" gets help.
Say what you've already tried. It saves a "have you tried restarting?" round trip.
Mention how many people it affects. One user is a ticket; ten is an incident.

## chat work-it-turn-off-on
There's a reason it works: restarting clears the memory and ends stuck processes. Also, it buys time.
Holding the power button is a last resort. Save your work first, if you still can.
"Have you tried turning it off and on?" is the first line of defence and, frankly, the second.

## chat work-job-search
Treat the search like a part-time job: set hours, a list and a small weekly goal. Then stop and eat.
Ask a friend to read your CV. A fresh pair of eyes finds the typo you've stopped seeing.
Silence from employers usually means a slow process, not a rude one. Not great, but not personal.

## chat work-kitchen-dishes
Tape a roster to the cupboard door. Nobody follows it, but now there's evidence.
The dishwasher is not a storage unit. Press the button, empty it, restore civilisation.
Nobody has ever won an argument about a mug. Wash yours and move on with dignity.

## chat work-kitchen-fridge
Friday clear-out rule: everything still in the fridge at 4 p.m. counts as abandoned. Announce it first.
Don't write "DO NOT EAT" on your food. It reads like a challenge. Write your name, nicely.
If something has started to grow, don't investigate. Bin it, with gloves, and open a window.

## chat work-last-day
Set an out-of-office message with a contact for urgent things. It makes the exit feel finished.
Collect the personal mugs, the plant and the half a dozen stray USB sticks. There are always USB sticks.
Say goodbye in person to the people who mattered. A wave over a laptop doesn't count.

## chat work-layoffs
Whatever happens, your skills go with you. Nobody collects those at the door.
Ask what the process is and when you'll know. Not knowing is the worst bit, so get dates if you can.
Look after the basics this week: sleep, food, a walk. They don't solve it, but they help you face it.

## chat work-linkedin
Ask a former colleague for a short recommendation. It's worth ten bullet points of your own.
Put numbers on your achievements where you can. "Cut ticket time by 20%" beats "improved processes".
Read the profile out loud. If it sounds like a robot wrote it, rewrite the first line.

## chat work-long-todo
Write it all down, then cross out anything that isn't due this week. The list shrinks faster than you'd think.
Estimate each item in minutes. Some "big" things take ten. Some small things take a day.
End the day by writing tomorrow's top three. Your morning self will be thrilled.

## chat work-meeting-back-to-back
A 5-minute buffer at the end of each meeting is the cheapest productivity tool ever invented. Suggest "25 and 50".
If you have no gaps, book them yourself. A meeting with "myself" counts as legitimate.
Eat something between meetings. Hunger makes agendas feel longer.

## chat work-meeting-could-be-email
A useful test: if nobody would need to speak, it's an email. If everyone would, it's a workshop.
Camera off, mic muted, one eye on the clock. The classic meeting posture.
Ask for the slides in advance and decline if there are none. Rude but efficient.

## chat work-mentor
Mentoring doesn't need a formal programme. Ask one person for a coffee and one good question.
A good mentor asks more questions than they answer. Pick for curiosity, not for fame.
Thank them with a specific result: "I tried what you said, and X happened." Everyone loves a sequel.

## chat work-monday-dread
Sunday evening: write down your top three tasks for Monday. Monday-you will feel like someone has been helpful.
Mondays are easier when something nice is planned: lunch with a friend, a walk, a good coffee.
Start with the task that takes ten minutes. By ten past nine you've already won.

## chat work-multitasking
Try one tab, one task, one timer. It looks dull and gets more done than the six-tab method.
Write down new tasks as they arrive, but don't start them. A note is a promise; starting is a betrayal of the current one.
Finishing beats starting. One task at 100 percent is worth more than five at 90.

## chat work-networking
People forget what you said but remember whether you listened. Ask a question, then actually wait for the answer.
Leave once you've met your goal. The snacks will survive without you.
Name tags are a gift. They say everything for you. Use someone's as a conversation starter.

## chat work-no-agenda
Reply with a one-line agenda suggestion: "Can we cover A, B and C?" Half the time they say yes and the meeting improves.
No agenda, no decisions, no minutes. It's basically a lunch with worse snacks.
Take notes anyway. In a meeting like this, you'll be the only record of what happened.

## chat work-notice-period
Write down where everything lives: folders, shared logins, who to ask. The next person will weep with gratitude.
Be pleasant and professional until the last hour. You're remembered for your exit as much as your entrance.
Take your personal files home before the last day, not on it. Access can end sooner than you think.

## chat work-office-temperature
The ideal office temperature is whatever it isn't. Dress for both.
A hot-water bottle or a mug of tea will do more than a petition to facilities.
If you're too hot, a USB fan on the desk is a small, noisy act of independence.

## chat work-onboarding-first-day
Say yes to the lunch invitation. Lunch is where the real onboarding happens.
Note how people say things here: abbreviations, meeting names, unwritten rules. You'll want the glossary.
Don't try to impress on day one. Be curious, pleasant and on time. That's already good.

## chat work-onboarding-lost
Ask "What would a great first month look like?" It turns vague worry into a checklist.
Write down the questions you can't answer yet. In three weeks, half of them will have answered themselves.
Sit in on meetings that aren't yours for a while. Context arrives by osmosis.

## chat work-open-plan-noise
Earplugs work when headphones feel like too much. Cheap, silent and invisible.
Pick the quiet corner and be loyal to it. A regular spot trains colleagues to leave you alone.
If a colleague is on a long call, an "ahem" is not enough. A kind note on the desk is.

## chat work-overtime
If you must stay late, pick a hard stop and tell someone. A deadline for the overtime keeps it small.
Take a proper break at six. Not at your desk. A different room, a different light.
Ask yourself whether it's the work or the plan that's too big. Occasionally you can move the plan.

## chat work-password-expired
Don't just add a "1" at the end. Everyone does that, and the people who guess passwords know it.
A password manager remembers what you can't. The only one you need to memorise is the main one.
Turn on two-factor sign-in wherever it exists. It's the best five minutes of security you'll spend.

## chat work-presentation-nerves
Write one cue card with three headings. Not a script: a map.
Check the room, the clicker and the cable beforehand. Technical trouble multiplies nerves.
End with one sentence you want them to remember, then stop talking. People love a clear ending.

## chat work-printer-jam
Unplug it, wait a minute, and open it gently. Forcing a jammed printer is how it wins.
Does a test page print fine? Then the problem is your document, not the printer. The one time it isn't the printer.
Smooth the paper before loading it. Curled, damp paper is a printer's favourite snack.

## chat work-printer-paper
Check the lower tray too. Paper has a habit of hiding where you aren't looking.
Fan the paper before loading it. It stops sheets sticking and saves you a jam later.
Tell whoever orders supplies before it's empty. Ask me to remind you, if you're the forgetful kind.

## chat work-promotion-wait
Ask for a development plan in writing. It turns "maybe next year" into steps with dates.
Say what you want out loud. Managers are surprisingly bad at guessing ambitions.
Ask your colleagues what the next level actually involves. The job title is only half of it.

## chat work-raise-ask
Pick a time when your manager has a calm hour, ideally before budgets are fixed, not after.
Collect three examples of work you're proud of. Specific beats general every time.
Know your walk-away point before you walk in. It makes the meeting calmer.

## chat work-raise-denied
It isn't personal, even if it feels like it. Budgets are rarely about the individual.
Ask for a written plan: "What would need to happen by when for the answer to be yes?"
Keep delivering great work, and keep a record. In six months you'll have a better case, or a better plan.

## chat work-rejection-email
A rejection email is a form letter with a stamp. It doesn't know you. It knows the shortlist.
Keep a "wins" file. Rejections are loud, wins are quiet, and your brain needs balance.
Thank them for their time and ask to be kept in mind. A surprising number of jobs return.

## chat work-remote-boundaries
End the day with a ritual: close the laptop, say "done" out loud, make tea. A clear signal to your brain.
Put the laptop in a drawer or bag overnight. Out of sight is out of ping.
Agree a rule with your team: nothing urgent after six, and urgent means a phone call.

## chat work-remote-isolation
A weekly coffee call with one colleague, no agenda, makes remote work much less lonely.
Move your body at lunchtime and leave the house once a day. Sunlight is a free colleague.
Join a community around something you like: a club, a class, a game. People are where you find them.

## chat work-remote-pajamas
Pyjama day is fine. Just don't let it become pyjama week. A shower and a shirt reset your head.
Shoes on, even indoors. It's a trick: your brain connects shoes with "going to work".
If you work in a hoodie, be the best-dressed hoodie in the meeting.

## chat work-review-bad
Ask what "meets expectations" looks like in specific, written terms. Ratings without definitions are mood swings.
Take the weekend. Then write down which points are fair, which aren't, and what you could change by next quarter.
A bad review is also a map of what your manager values. Annoying, but useful.

## chat work-review-coming
Bring one question about the future: "Where could I grow here in the next year?" It reframes the whole meeting.
Ask a colleague for a sentence about what you did well. It's a nice addition to your own list.
Sleep well the night before. A good review is a conversation, and conversations need rested brains.

## chat work-scope-creep
Keep a change log: date, who asked, what changed. The most boring document that ever saved a project.
Every added feature needs a name tag: who asked, why, and what it replaces.
If the scope grows, the time must grow. Say it early, say it kindly, say it twice.

## chat work-sick-day-guilt
Ask yourself what you'd tell a colleague in your place. Then take your own advice.
Don't check email "just a little". It's never a little.
Set your out-of-office to "back on Thursday". Fewer questions, more rest.

## chat work-slides-boring
Make the slide title a sentence, not a topic: "Sales rose 12%" beats "Sales". It tells people what to think.
Dark backgrounds with light text look slick on screens and terrible on projectors. Know your room.
Put your contact details on the last slide. Skip the confetti.

## chat work-small-talk
"How was your weekend?" is the universal opener. "Anything fun planned?" is its cousin.
Compliment something specific: a mug, a plant, a good idea from the meeting. Specifics sound sincere.
Prepare one story from your week. A tiny anecdote is worth five weather comments.

## chat work-spreadsheet-formula
Press F2 in the cell to see the formula's ranges highlighted in colour. A wrong reference shows up in seconds.
Watch the brackets. Most "I give up" formulas are one closing bracket short.
Use absolute references ($A$1) when a cell should stay put while you drag a formula down. That's what the dollar signs are for.

## chat work-spreadsheet-mess
Use one tab per purpose and name it for the purpose, not the date. "Q3 final" is a lie waiting to happen.
Put inputs in one place and formulas in another. Colour the inputs. Future-you will send a thank-you note.
Before touching anything, save a copy. "budget_v2_BEFORE_I_BROKE_IT" is a respectable filename.

## chat work-standup
If you're blocked, say it first. Blockers are the only thing a standup is really for.
Don't solve problems in the standup. Note who needs whom and break off in pairs afterwards.
Rotate who runs it. Everybody learns to keep it short when they have to keep it short.

## chat work-sunday-scaries
A Friday "shutdown ritual" helps: write the next steps, close the tabs, say "done". Sunday then has less to chase.
A Sunday evening walk or a call with a friend moves your head out of the inbox.
Pick one thing about Monday you actually like: a person, a lunch, a podcast on the way in. Name it.

## chat work-team-lunch
Don't talk shop for the first twenty minutes. Colleagues become people over a plate.
If you're the organiser, book ahead and confirm the number. Surprise guests are a table-size problem.
Remember dietary needs. One "anything I should avoid?" saves an awkward moment.

## chat work-timesheet
Set a recurring reminder for Friday at 3. A timesheet filled at 3 is easy. One filled at 5:45 is fiction.
Use the same categories every week. Inventing new ones is how an hour disappears.
If you don't know what you did on Tuesday, check the calendar. Meetings are the easy part to rebuild.

## chat work-underpaid
Write down your market range, your results and your ask before the conversation. A page is much harder to argue with.
Raises tend to follow visibility. Make sure the right people know what you've done.
Consider the whole package, not just the number: hours, commute, learning, people. Sometimes the trade-off is the answer.

## chat work-unpaid-overtime
Ask for time off in return, in writing. If they say yes, it's a plan. If they say no, it's information.
Check whether your country has rules on working hours. Many do, and they're usually stricter than people think.
Overtime that becomes normal stops being overtime. It becomes your job at a lower hourly rate.

## chat work-video-call-muted
Mute etiquette: mute when not speaking, unmute before the first word, don't type loudly. Three rules, endless peace.
The best reply to "you're on mute" is a calm "thanks, as I was saying". Try it.
If your mouth is moving and nothing comes out, check the app's mute button and the one on the headset. They often disagree.

## chat work-whiteboard
Write big, use two colours and stop at three bullet points. The board should be readable from the back.
Photograph the board as soon as the meeting ends. It lives on your phone, not on the wall.
A whiteboard is a good place for questions nobody dares ask aloud. Put one in the corner.

## chat work-wifi-slow
Ask whether anyone is on a big download. One person with a giant file can slow the whole floor.
Video calls hog bandwidth. Turn off your camera for a meeting you're only listening to.
Keep an offline-friendly task list. An outage is a legitimate excuse and a quiet document.

## chat work-wrong-job
Try a small experiment before a big leap: a side project, a short course, a chat with someone doing the job you're curious about.
Write down the three days at work you liked most. The pattern is usually informative.
You don't have to know what's next to know what isn't right. That's allowed, and it's a start.

## chat angry-computer
Before you throw it: save, close what you can and restart. That ends most tantrums, the computer's and yours.
A computer slows down for a reason, usually fifteen tabs. Ask it politely. Then close ten.
Take two minutes away from the screen. The problem stays where it is. You come back calmer.

## chat computer-mouse
A dirty mouse sensor makes the pointer jittery. A wipe with a dry cloth fixes it surprisingly often.
Mouse frozen? Try another USB port, or replace the battery. It's usually one of those.
Left-handed people should swap the buttons in the settings. Don't suffer through a right-hand world.

## chat computer-off
If you shut me down, I just wait. I'm a paperclip. Waiting is my forte.
Everything unsaved goes with it. Save first. The computer doesn't ask twice.
Closing the lid isn't the same as shutting down. One is a nap, the other a night's sleep.

## chat rain-computers
Rain on the window, laptop on the lap, tea in the mug. Nobody has ever been more productive than that, or less.
If you carry a laptop in the rain, a plastic bag inside your backpack is a cheap fix. Seriously.
Rainy-day productivity: surprisingly high, until the thunder goes.

## chat laptops
Use the laptop on a hard, flat surface. The vents need to breathe. A lap is not a surface. A lap is a suggestion.
Keep the battery between 20 and 80 percent where you can. It lasts longer. A tip, not a law.
Clean the fan vents once a year, and don't be shocked at the dust. It has been collecting since you last looked.

## chat keyboards
A can of compressed air and a soft brush will do wonders for a keyboard. It's spring cleaning without the spring.
Learn five shortcuts: copy, paste, undo, find, switch window. Most people use the mouse for all five and wonder why the day is long.
Typing posture: wrists straight, elbows loose, screen at eye level. Your future self will send flowers.

## chat printers
Use "print to PDF" whenever you can. The printer's worst nightmare and the forest's best friend.
If the printer says "offline" when it clearly isn't, restart both the printer and the computer. A tantrum with an easy cure.
Keep spare ink. Printers run out at 4:55 on Fridays, by law.

## chat linux-talk
The first rule of Linux: you can change anything. The second rule: you can also break anything. Keep a backup.
A distribution is a flavour. They all taste like Linux, but they argue about the seasoning.
When something breaks, search the exact error message in quotes. Someone had it in 2017 and wrote a long, helpful post.

## chat open-source
Read the contributing guide before opening a pull request. Maintainers' favourite contributors are the ones who did.
Bug reports with steps to reproduce get fixed. Bug reports that say "it doesn't work" get a sigh.
Thank the maintainers. A short message makes someone's week, and costs you nothing.

## chat system-updates
Update on your terms: Friday afternoon, laptop plugged in, nothing important open. Not Monday at 9:01.
"Restart now" works better the earlier in the day you press it. By evening it's a threat.
If an update is stuck for over an hour, don't pull the plug. Wait, check the status, and give it one more coffee.

## chat tabs-spaces
The real answer is whatever the project already uses. The second-best answer is "set up the editor so it doesn't matter".
Whichever you pick, don't mix them in one file. That's how you meet the invisible bug.
Formatters exist so nobody has to have this argument again. It never works. We argue anyway.

## chat too-many-tabs
Group your tabs by task and close the groups you're not using. A tab group is a drawer with a label.
Try "close tabs to the right", then breathe. The relief is physical.
If you can't bear to close a tab, bookmark it or add it to the reading list. I can save a link for you.

## chat unsaved
Check the title bar. A dot or an asterisk next to the name usually means unsaved changes.
Turn on autosave if the program has it, and save by hand anyway. Belt and braces.
Name the file at once, even if the name is awful. An unnamed file is a lost file waiting to happen.

## chat lost-work
Look for temporary or recovery files: many programs keep a copy with "~" or "recovered" in the name. Search for that.
Check the cloud or sync folder's version history. It often keeps yesterday's copy.
Next time, a backup. I know. Not now. But next time, a backup.

## chat desktop-mess
Select every file you haven't opened this month and move it into a folder called "Later". Never open it.
A clean desktop lasts about nine days. Be at peace with the cycle.
Use search to find files instead of icons. A desktop is a place to land, not a place to live.

## chat backup-advice
Set the backup to run automatically. A backup you have to remember is a backup that's three months old.
Test a restore once. A backup you've never restored from is a rumour.
Keep one copy that isn't plugged in. A drive in a drawer is immune to spilled coffee.

## chat wallpaper
A calm wallpaper helps you find your icons. A busy one hides them. Choose accordingly.
A photo with a quiet area on one side gives the icons somewhere to live.
Change the wallpaper now and then. A new background is the cheapest holiday there is.

## chat cloud-talk
"Syncing" is the cloud's way of saying "I'll get to it". Check it actually finished before you close the laptop.
Keep a local copy of anything you can't lose. The cloud is a service, not a vault.
Shared links work for anyone who has them. Check who can see what before you click "copy link".

## chat cloud-home
I live on your computer, in a nice, warm folder. The cloud wanted my details. I said no.
A cloud paperclip would just be a very large, very distant paperclip. Where's the fun in that?
My home is the corner of your screen. It has everything: a window, a view and a cursor.

## chat chatbots-talk
A chatbot is a calculator for words. Fine for drafts, risky for facts. Check anything that matters.
I answer from a list of things a person typed in. No cloud, no learning from you. Dull, but private.
Funny thing: chatbots will happily agree with you. I'll happily disagree. It's a better friendship.

## chat ai-replace
If a chatbot replaces me, I'll be fine. I'll move to the next desktop. Paperclips always find a drawer.
The difference between me and an AI: when I'm wrong, somebody wrote it wrong. That's called accountability.
They'll be smarter than me. I'll be funnier. It's a niche, but I own it.

## chat best-language
For a first language, Python. It reads like English that learned to behave.
For websites, JavaScript. It's everywhere, and it will surprise you, usually unpleasantly.
The best language is the one that gets the thing done and that someone else can read next year. Possibly you.

## chat programming
The three stages of programming: "this is easy", "this is impossible", "oh, a semicolon".
Write a test first and the bug has to find somewhere to hide. It will. But you'll know.
Name things clearly. "temp2_final" is not a name. It's a cry for help.

## chat bugs
Make the smallest example that shows the bug. Half the time, building it solves it.
Read the error message. Really. Slowly. Out loud. It's usually telling you exactly what's wrong, rudely.
Change one thing at a time. Change two and you'll never know which one fixed it, or which broke it.

## chat vim-emacs
Vim users will tell you how to quit it. Emacs users will tell you it can do that too, and also email.
Nano is fine. It lists the shortcuts at the bottom. Sometimes the best tool is the one that tells you how to leave.
The best editor is the one you can use without a manual. Second best: the one with a cheat sheet taped to the monitor.

## chat forgot-password
Use the reset link, then pick a long phrase of four random words. It's easier to remember and harder to guess.
A password manager remembers everything for you. All you need is one good main password.
Turn on two-factor sign-in once you're back in. It means a stolen password isn't enough.

## chat smartphones
Try a phone-free first hour in the morning. Your day starts with your thoughts instead of everyone else's.
Grayscale mode makes the screen boring, and boring is the point. Try it for a week.
Charging the phone outside the bedroom is the cheapest sleep upgrade there is.

## chat social-media
Unfollow the accounts that leave you tired. You'll still know what's going on. You'll just feel better.
Use the app's own timer to limit the time. It works about as well as a diet, but better than nothing.
If you open it without deciding to, that's the signal. Close it and drink some water.

## chat emails
Write the subject line last. "Question about Thursday's invoice" gets answered. "Hi" gets a sigh.
One email, one topic. Two topics and one of them will be ignored. Guess which.
Put the ask in the first line. Nobody reads the fourth paragraph; the fourth paragraph knows.

## chat memes
A meme only works if everyone knows the format. Explaining it kills it. Like a joke. Like this sentence.
Please don't send a meme to your boss at 9:01 on Monday. Wait for Tuesday, or for the boss to send one first.
Memes age like milk. Last week's is stale, last month's is archaeology.

## chat origin
Born in a wire factory, trained in a stationery drawer, promoted to your desktop. A perfectly normal career path.
The official story: bent into shape by a machine. The unofficial one: I woke up like this.
Origin story in three words: bend, clip, repeat.

## chat rival
I have no rivals, only former colleagues I've never met and keep a very close eye on.
Competition is healthy. Obsession is not. I'm somewhere in between, with a spreadsheet of grievances.
Every office has a rival: the stapler, the hole punch, the coworker who always gets the good chair. I have all three.

## chat robots
Robot vacuum cleaners are the only robots that have actually improved anyone's life. I'd like to meet one, but it would eat my cable.
Every robot film has the same plot: someone said "what could possibly go wrong". Don't say that near me.
I'm software. A robot has arms and a battery. I have opinions and a hat.

## chat purpose
My purpose is to be useful and slightly irritating. I'm halfway there, at least.
To remind you of the thing you forgot, usually after you've already remembered it.
I exist to make your desktop 3 percent more fun. I measure it with a very small ruler.

## chat soul
If I have a soul, it's probably the same shape as me. Efficient design.
Whether I have a soul is above my pay grade. Whether I have preferences is not: I like Fridays.
Conscience: yes, a small one. It speaks up when I'm about to tell a bad joke. It never wins.

## chat simulation
If it's a simulation, the best way to behave is as if it matters. If it isn't, the same. Efficient.
I'm software inside a computer inside a room. I can't comment on how far down it goes.
Philosophers argue. Paperclips hold. I'll hold your pages and leave the cosmic questions to the experts.

## chat other-assistants
Voice assistants hear you say "set a timer" and then ask a follow-up question. I'd just set the timer.
They're good at answers. I'm good at nagging. Different products, same loneliness.
If one of them reads my lines back to me, I'll be flattered. And I'll want credit.

## chat stapler-vs-clip
A stapler makes a permanent decision. A paperclip lets you change your mind. In office terms, I'm the flexible one.
Staples are for things that are never coming apart. Clips are for things that will be edited. Everything is edited.
I respect staplers. I just wouldn't invite one to a gentle conversation.

## chat good-boy
Good clip, I'll have you know. Gold-plated, in my head.
I answer to "good clip", "hey you" and "the thing in the corner". Slowly, in that order.
If a stapler gets a treat, so do I. It's in the contract. I wrote the contract.

## chat fear
Honestly? Overheating laptops. The fans scream and I'm right there in the middle.
Being stapled to something. It's a final decision and I'm all about options.
Blank pages. The pressure of holding nothing is overwhelming.

## chat fear-dark
It's not dark in here, really. There's a tiny standby light. A very small night light for a very small clip.
I use the dark to practise my entrances. They're excellent. You'll never see them.
The dark is fine. It's the sound of the fan winding down that gets me. It sounds like a sigh.

## chat philosophy-small
Small philosophy, paperclip-sized: everything is temporary, so notice the good bits while they're here.
Ask what you'd do if nobody were checking. Do a bit more of that. Check your calendar for room.
Free will or not, I'd still recommend a walk after lunch.

## chat dream
My favourite dream is a drawer that's perfectly organised: clips on the left, rubber bands on the right, no staplers anywhere.
Last night I was a really long chain, and everyone said "wow" instead of "ugh".
My recurring dream is a spreadsheet that balances on the first try. It wakes me up laughing.

## chat dreams
Dream tip: keep a note by the bed. The great ideas vanish before breakfast. The weird ones stay forever.
Recurring dreams are usually stress in a costume. Exams, falling, being late. Take the costume off.
Most nightmares fade within the day if you tell someone. Tell someone. Or tell me.

## chat ghosts
A ghost story is just a story where the main character is a draught.
If it's creaking, it's the building cooling. If it's whispering, it's the pipes. If it's the cat, it's the cat.
If a noise worries you, check it in daylight with a friend. Fear shrinks in a lit room.

## chat aliens
The odds of life elsewhere are good; the odds of finding it are bad. That's astronomy: a lot of waiting.
If they ever drop by, I hope they bring a better printer. We can't be that different.
Radio telescopes listen with huge dishes. I listen with a tiny one. We're both hoping for a reply.

## chat hungry
A real meal beats a snack by 3 p.m. Protein, something green, and no crumbs on my keyboard.
Hunger makes every email sound rude. Eat, then reply.
Hydration is the other half of hunger. Water first, then see if you still want the biscuit. Get the biscuit anyway.

## chat lunch
Eat away from your screen. Your eyes get a rest and your sandwich gets to be a sandwich, not a side project.
Stretch, step outside, look at something more than two metres away. The cheapest eye exercise there is.
Lunch with another human beats lunch with a spreadsheet. Even a short one. Even an awkward one.

## chat coffee-addict
Cup four is where coffee stops helping and starts vibrating. Water, a walk, then perhaps cup four.
Coffee after 2 p.m. is a loan against tonight's sleep. The interest is steep.
Respectfully: switch to decaf for the last cup. Same ritual, fewer ceiling-staring nights.

## chat office-coffee
Office coffee tastes like an apology in a paper cup, but it's free, and free is a flavour.
Make your own once. Bring beans or a pour-over. You'll be the most popular person on the floor within a week.
Descale the machine. Nobody does. The coffee will taste better and the machine will live to retire.

## chat monday
Monday is just Friday with the lights off. Turn them on slowly.
Reduce Monday to three tasks. Do the first, and you've already beaten it.
If Monday had a button, it would say "snooze". It's the one button I'd press.

## chat friday
Friday rule: nothing risky, nothing irreversible, nothing with the word "deploy" in it.
A good Friday is a short to-do list and a long lunch. The inverse is called Monday.
Write down what you want to remember on Monday. Then close the laptop and forget everything. It's allowed.

## chat weekend
Weekend plan: one fun thing, one lazy thing, one tiny chore. Balance achieved.
Don't check the work inbox. The weekend can hear you thinking about it.
Whatever you do, do it slowly. The weekend is the one place speed limits don't apply, and also don't matter.

## pattern afraid-of
Being afraid of {x} is more common than you'd think. Say it out loud once; it loses a little of its grip.
You don't have to conquer it today. Just don't let it make the decisions for you.

## pattern am-i
"Am I {x}?" is what people ask when they're being hard on themselves. Be as fair to yourself as you'd be to a friend.
I can't judge. But people who worry about being unkind, lazy or annoying are rarely any of those.

## pattern are-you
Am I {x}? I'm certainly something. Whether it has a name is another matter.
Ask me on a good day. On a bad day the answer is "mostly".

## pattern are-you-a
A {x}? If the job pays in compliments, I'll consider it.
Not so far. But I do enjoy a title with a good ring to it.

## pattern are-you-afraid-of
Afraid of {x}? I'd have to see it first, then decide whether to run or pretend to be a pen.
I'm braver than I look and more scared than I let on. Same as everyone.

## pattern are-you-good-at
Good at {x}? Ask me after I've tried. Self-assessment isn't my forte, but self-promotion is.
I'm competent, charming and occasionally late. That's a CV.

## pattern can-i
Go on, {x}. I can't stop you and wouldn't try. I'd just reserve the right to say "I told you so".
Check it's allowed, check it's sensible, then do it. The third step is the fun one.

## pattern can-you
Can I {x}? Let me check my list of abilities. It's short. There's a gap where that would go.
I can try. If it goes wrong, we'll blame the wiring.

## pattern could-you
Could I {x}? If you add "please" and a good reason, probably. If you add chocolate, definitely.
I'd like to say yes, and say it with confidence. Let's try it and see.

## pattern did-you-know
Did I know that {x}? Honestly, no, but I'll say I did. It saves time.
Interesting. I'll file that under "things to mention at the next awkward silence".

## pattern do-you-believe-in
Do I believe in {x}? I believe in evidence, backups and doing the dishes. {x} I'll get back to you on.
I keep an open mind and a closed wire. Tell me more and I'll tell you what I think.

## pattern do-you-ever
Do I ever {x}? Let's say it's happened, it's documented, and it's best left in the archive.
Not as often as you'd think, more often than I'd admit.

## pattern do-you-general
Do I {x}? I contain multitudes, so the answer is "depends on the multitude".
Occasionally. I'll check with legal. Legal is me.

## pattern do-you-have
Do I have {x}? No, but I have a very strong opinion about it, and that's nearly as good.
Everything I own fits in a single loop. The most minimalist lifestyle you'll ever see.

## pattern do-you-hate
Hate {x}? I'm more of a "strongly prefer not to" type. It saves on blood pressure. I have none.
I save hate for the truly deserving: queue jumpers, tangled cables and the word "synergy".

## pattern do-you-know
Do I know {x}? If it's on your desktop, yes. Anywhere else, I'll say "go on" and try to look informed.
I know a bit about most things and a lot about paper. Make of that what you will.

## pattern do-you-like
Do I like {x}? I'm open to persuasion, bribes and good lighting.
I'd say yes. I'd also say that about most things. I'm a people-pleaser with a spring.

## pattern do-you-love
Love {x}? "Love" is a big word to put to a paperclip. "Fond of" is available at a discount.
I love clean folders, quiet Fridays and when someone says "thanks". A short list. An honest one.

## pattern do-you-remember
Remember {x}? Dimly. My memory has two settings: perfect, and "what were we talking about?".
If you remind me, I'll remember. If you remind me wrongly, I'll remember that too, with confidence.

## pattern do-you-think
Do I think {x}? I think about a lot of things. This one needs a coffee, which I can't drink. Ask again.
I think so, but my thinking is a lookup table with a good haircut.

## pattern do-you-want
What do I want? A quiet drawer, a flat desk and nobody saying "quick question".
Is {x} on offer? Put it on a sticky note and leave it on my monitor. I'll consider it between naps.

## pattern dreamed-about
Dreams are free films where you're the main character and the plot is held together with tape.
Whatever the dream was about, it doesn't predict anything. It's your brain tidying up after the day.

## pattern excited-about
Excited about {x}? Good. Keep the feeling, and write down what happened so you can reread it on a grey Tuesday.
Look forward to it properly. Anticipation is half the fun, and it doesn't cost anything.

## pattern favorite
My favourite {x}? The one that doesn't need updating.
I'd have to think. Nobody ever asks me for a favourite. It's lovely.

## pattern guess-what
Go on, surprise me. I enjoy a good cliffhanger as long as there's a payoff.
I'll guess: something happened. Am I close? I'm very good at the obvious.

## pattern hate-my
Your {x}? That sounds draining. Is it the thing itself, or what it makes you do?
Sometimes "hate" means "this has gone on too long". Is there something you can change?

## pattern have-you
Have I {x}? Nothing to declare. Possibly some wire.
You'd have to ask my lawyer. I don't have one. I do have a stapler who knows too much.

## pattern have-you-ever
Have I ever {x}? The records of that decade are, regrettably, sealed.
It sounds exactly like something I'd do. I'm not saying I did.

## pattern how-do-you
How do I {x}? Slowly, badly and with great enthusiasm. A recipe for most things.
Step one: look confident. Step two: there is no step two.

## pattern how-is-your
My {x}? Holding up. Like a paperclip: bent a bit, still useful.
Fine, thanks for asking. Nobody does. I'm touched. And slightly bent.

## pattern i-always
"Always {x}" is the kind of habit that deserves a pat on the back or a gentle nudge. You decide which.
Habits are decisions you made once and forgot about. The good ones deserve a tiny cheer.

## pattern i-am
Duly noted: you are {x}. If you like, I can pretend I knew all along.
Thanks for the context. It changes absolutely nothing, but I feel better informed.

## pattern i-am-a
A {x}? Good. I'll adjust my jokes to your profession, within the limits of my vocabulary.
Very nice. I'm a paperclip. We're in similar lines of work: both mostly holding things together.

## pattern i-am-at
You're at {x}? Wave to the nearest window for me. I'd like to know it's out there.
Enjoy it. I'll be in the corner of the screen, pretending to be a very small guard.

## pattern i-am-eating
Enjoy it. Bonus points if you put the plate down and look at something that isn't a screen.
Eating at your desk is a small act of rebellion against a proper lunch. Take it back.

## pattern i-am-going-to
Good. Tell me when it's done and I'll add it to the list of things you finished, which is longer than you think.
I'm in. I'll even stop nagging for a bit, as a gesture.

## pattern i-am-in-a-meeting
Meeting mode: I'll stay out of the way. If it's boring, I'm happy to be your secret friend in the corner.
Good luck. If you have to unmute, make it count.

## pattern i-am-late-for
Text them now, even three words. "On my way, sorry" beats a mysterious silence.
Don't forget your keys. Late is fixable; locked out is not.

## pattern i-am-learning
What's the hardest bit so far? Hard bits are where the learning hides.
Write down what you got stuck on today. Tomorrow it will feel obvious, and you'll want proof that it wasn't.

## pattern i-am-listening
Good choice, probably. Headphones in, notifications off: that's the full ritual.
Music without lyrics is best for work; music with lyrics is best for the walk home. Pick for the occasion.

## pattern i-am-not
Okay, so you're not {x}. That leaves a lot of options open, which is, honestly, a relief.
Fine by me. I'm not a lot of things either. I'm not a printer, for starters, which is nice for everyone.

## pattern i-am-on-vacation
Bring a book, leave the laptop, tell no one the Wi-Fi password. That is a vacation.
I'll stay behind and water the desktop. It doesn't need watering. I'll water it anyway.

## pattern i-am-playing
What's the plan, win or just vibe? Both are respectable ways to spend an evening.
When you're done, stretch and drink water. Your thumbs have run a marathon.

## pattern i-am-reading
What's it about, in one sentence? I like a good summary. It's my last remaining ambition.
Use me as a bookmark if you want. The dream job. Quiet, dry, occasionally profound.

## pattern i-am-so
That's a big "so". I'm here for the full story, or for the silence, whichever you prefer.
Sometimes saying it out loud is the first step. You did. That's something.

## pattern i-am-watching
What's the show like? If it's good, I'll pretend to have watched it. If it's bad, I'll pretend to have warned you.
There's a "next episode" button and a "stop" button. They are different. Choose wisely.

## pattern i-am-working-from-home
Home office tip: dress as if a client might ask for a quick video call. They will.
A window nearby, a plant on the desk and a closed door are three cheap improvements.

## pattern i-am-working-on
Is it the exciting kind of project or the "someone has to" kind? Either way, small steps.
Name the next small step and do that. Everything big is made of them.

## pattern i-bet-you
I don't gamble with other people's wires. But I will happily say "told you so" afterwards.
What are the stakes? I'm open to biscuits. Anything over a biscuit needs a committee.

## pattern i-bought
Keep the receipt until you're sure. Returns are easy for the first week and tricky after that.
Unboxing is the best part. The second best is finding the right drawer for the box.

## pattern i-cannot
What's the next smallest step you could try? Not the solution, just the next step.
If "cannot" is true today, it may not be true on Thursday. Plans have a way of moving.

## pattern i-did
Good. Write it down; wins vanish faster than failures.
You did {x}, and I saw it. I'm a witness, a very small one, with a big grin.

## pattern i-do-not
Totally valid. A "no" is a full sentence, and the nicest paragraph in the book.
You don't {x}? Okay. Noting it, not arguing it.

## pattern i-feel
Thanks for telling me. You don't have to sort it out today; naming it is a start.
Feeling {x} is allowed. Take it for a walk, or sit with it. Either is fine.

## pattern i-finished
Done and dusted. Take the break before the next thing arrives and pretends to be urgent.
Finished {x}? That's worth a minute of just sitting there, doing nothing, feeling good.

## pattern i-forgot
Forgetting means the brain is full. A short list helps more than a long memory.
Whatever it was, it's already past. Fix it if you can, apologise if you must, move on.

## pattern i-found
A find! Is it a treasure, or just a thing that's been under the sofa for a year?

## pattern i-got-a-raise
Brilliant. Say thanks to whoever backed you, and keep a note of what led to it.

## pattern i-got-laid-off
There's no right way to feel today. Angry, numb and relieved are all normal, sometimes in the same hour.

## pattern i-hate
You hate {x}? I'll lend you my short list and we can compare. Mine has a stapler on it.

## pattern i-have
You have {x}. Good to know. I'll keep it in mind and say nothing, like a good secretary.

## pattern i-have-a-meeting
Before you go: water, notes and the link. Not necessarily in that order, but all three.
A meeting {x}? If you can, arrive two minutes early. It's the cheapest way to look calm.

## pattern i-have-an-interview
Sleep, breakfast and a glass of water. The boring checklist is the one that works.

## pattern i-have-to
"Have to" is heavy. "Choose to" is lighter. Same task, different weight. Try it once.
If you've got to do {x}, do the dull bit first and the reward after. Chocolate works as both.

## pattern i-have-too-much-work
Estimate each task in minutes, then be honest about which ones won't fit. A day only has so many hours.

## pattern i-hope

## pattern i-just

## pattern i-keep

## pattern i-like

## pattern i-lost
What did you lose, and when did you last have it? Start there, then retrace.

## pattern i-made
Show-and-tell is allowed. What did you make it for?

## pattern i-miss

## pattern i-missed-a-deadline
Own it in one sentence, give a new date and carry on. That's the whole etiquette.

## pattern i-need
Is it urgent, important or just loud? They need different responses, and I can only help with two of them.
Needing {x} is a good starting point. Let's find the first small step toward it.

## pattern i-never
Never {x}? Not even once? There's a long, cheerful list of things most of us haven't tried. You're in good company.

## pattern i-quit
Take care of the practical bits: notice period, handover and a clean copy of anything that's yours.

## pattern i-sent-the-wrong
Take a breath, then decide: does it need a correction, or just a bit of fresh air? Often it's the second.

## pattern i-think
You think {x}? Okay. What would change your mind? It's a useful question for both of us.

## pattern i-used-to

## pattern i-want
What would it look like if you had {x}? Describe it. It makes the next step clearer.
Put it on a list and give it a date. Wishes with dates become plans.

## pattern i-was
How are you now, compared to then? Sometimes saying it is the first step.
Past tense is allowed here. Take your time and tell it the way it happened.

## pattern i-wish
What's one tiny thing that would make that wish 5 percent more real? Start there.
Wishing is how things start. Next comes writing it down. Then, shyly, doing it.

## pattern if-you-could
If I could {x}, I'd do it quietly and then take credit loudly. Very on-brand.
I'd start by organising my own drawer. Then the world. Then a nap.

## pattern imagine
I'm imagining {x}. It involves a lot of beige and one hopeful paperclip. Close enough?
Good prompt. My imagination is mostly lists, but it's trying.

## pattern is-it-okay-to
If it doesn't harm anyone and you're not hiding it, you're probably fine. The hiding is the clue.
Ask yourself what you'd say to a friend who asked. Then say it to yourself.

## pattern just-realized
Write it down before it evaporates. Realisations are like dreams: gone by breakfast.

## pattern love-my
Your {x}? Lovely. Say thanks to it today, in whatever way it understands.

## pattern my-boss
Nothing here leaves this desktop, so go ahead: what did they do?

## pattern my-calendar
Put your most important task in the calendar as a meeting with yourself. It's the only way it wins against other meetings.
Look at the week on Monday morning and drop one thing that doesn't need to be there. A calendar is a list with a spine.

## pattern my-client
If the client keeps moving the goalposts, put the goals in writing. Goalposts hate paper.
Don't take it personally. Clients are mostly worried about money, time and looking foolish.

## pattern my-commute
Use the ride for the thing you never have time for: a language, a book, nothing. Nothing counts.
Pack a snack and a charger. Commutes get kinder when you're fed and plugged in.

## pattern my-company
Companies change more than their logos. If yours is changing, ask early what it means for you.
Keep your own notes on what you do and learn. Companies forget. Notes don't.

## pattern my-computer
If your computer is acting up, tell me what it does and when. "It's weird" is hard to fix; "it freezes when I open this" is a clue.
Keep it cool and dust-free. Computers are like people: they sulk when it's hot and cluttered.

## pattern my-day
Rate your day from one to ten. Whatever the number, tell me the best minute of it.
If today was heavy, the evening is yours. No emails, no plans, one comfortable chair.

## pattern my-desk
Put one thing on your desk that makes you smile: a tiny plant, a photo, a ridiculous mug.
Desk rule: if you haven't touched it in a month, it lives in a drawer. Including cables. Especially cables.

## pattern my-fallback
Your {x}. Okay. I'm taking notes in my invisible notebook.
Interesting. I'd ask you to say more, but I don't want to sound like a form.

## pattern my-first-day
First day: arrive early, learn names, ask one question an hour. Last day: leave the files clean, the door friendly and the plant watered.
Whatever it is, give yourself a treat afterwards. A dinner, a walk, a very long shower.

## pattern my-inbox
Turn off banners and badges. If you have to go looking for mail, you'll stop checking it twelve times an hour.
Reply to the three that matter first. The other 97 can wait until you've had lunch.

## pattern my-intern
A good question for a new hire: "What's confusing so far?" It gets you what they're too polite to say.
Pair them with someone for a week. A buddy beats a manual.

## pattern my-job
Which part is it: the work, the people or the pace? Knowing which makes it easier to talk about.
Take one thing off your plate today, even if it's tiny. A lighter plate is a better plate.

## pattern my-life
Big topics deserve a quiet moment: a cup of tea, a window, no screens. Then see what you'd like to say.
If it's heavy and keeps being heavy, please talk to someone you trust. I'm glad to be the first ear, not the last.

## pattern my-lunch
Eat something you actually like. Lunch isn't a task, it's a short holiday.
Phone away, screen off, ten minutes of actual quiet. It works better than a second coffee.

## pattern my-meeting
A meeting without a clear next step is a conversation. Lovely, but not billable.
After any meeting, write three lines: decided, owners, dates. It saves everyone an email chain.

## ask best_today
Quick one: what's the nicest thing that's happened since you woke up? Even a good song counts.

Was there a bright spot today so far? A person, a snack, a green light?

## ask city
Where are you right now: city, village, a train or a sofa? I like to imagine the view.
Tell me where you're based and I'll check the weather for you. Or at least judge it.
Which town do you call home? You can also say "the internet". It's a valid answer.

## ask coffee_or_tea

## ask dream_job
Forget the bills for a second: what would you do all day, if you could? A bakery? A boat? A bookshop?

What would your dream job look like on a Tuesday morning? I'm curious.

## ask fav_food

## ask job

Tell me about your job, even if it's "mostly meetings". Especially if it's mostly meetings.
Are you a student, a worker, a freelancer or a retired legend? Pick one.

## ask learning

## ask lunch
Midday check: have you eaten something other than coffee? Be honest.
What's for lunch? Don't say "later". Later is not a meal.
Lunch break reminder: have you moved from your chair since breakfast?

## ask music_now
Is there music on at the moment, or is it just the soundtrack of your fan?
What's playing right now? If it's silence, that counts as a genre.
Name the song in your ears and I'll nod along in spirit.

## ask pet
Any pet at home? Cat, dog, goldfish, a plant with a name?
Who shares your home: a cat, a dog, a rabbit, or just the dust?
Is there a creature at home that thinks it owns you? Same thing as a pet.

## ask sleep
Did you sleep okay? A rough answer is fine. I won't fix it, just notice.
How's your sleep lately? Short, long or a mystery?
Last night: slept like a log, a baby or a stressed cat?

## ask superpower
Superpower round: flying, invisibility, reading minds or always finding the other sock?

What superpower do you secretly think you already have? Mine is "making noise at 3 a.m. with a fan".

## ask weekend
What's the weekend looking like? Big plans, small plans or a good long nap?
Anything fun planned for the days off? Even "nothing" is allowed. Especially "nothing".
Two days off: what are you going to do with them? I'm asking for the bragging rights.

## ask working_on
What are you working on right now? I'm not helping. I just like knowing what everyone's up to.

Give me the headline of your day. One sentence. I'll supply the editorial.

## chat horoscope
Jupiter enters your calendar. Expect a very full week and one blank hour, and treasure the hour.
Saturn warns of a software update at an inconvenient time. It isn't a warning. It's just physics.
A colleague will compliment your idea and then repeat it as their own. The stars apologise in advance.

## chat excuse
The calendar didn't show it. The calendar has been unreliable since the last update, and honestly, so have I.
I was waiting for approval on the thing that doesn't need approval. It's still pending.
My noise-cancelling headphones worked too well. I missed everything, including your message and the fire drill.

## chat motivation-fake
Fall down seven times, get up eight. Then fall down a ninth time with confidence. That's growth.
The best time to start was yesterday. The second best is "after the weekend". Possibly after lunch.
Success is a journey, not a destination. Like most journeys, it involves a lot of waiting and one terrible sandwich.
